Διπολιώδη : schol. V (ext.) τὰ λεγόμενα Διάσια ταῦτα καὶ Διϊπόλεια [R: -λι- V]* οὕτως δὲ ἐλέγετο ἃ τῷ πολιεῖ Διὶ ἐθύετο [also 8.7" τὸ δὲ τεττίγων ὅτι ἀρχαῖον» τὸ τοὺς τέττιγας ἀναπλέκειν, τουτέστι κρωβύλους, ἢ ὅτι οἱ παλαιοὶ (inf.) κατὰ τὴν ἀναπλοκὴν τῶν τριχῶν χρυσῷ τέττιγι" τεκμήριον διὰ τὸ φαίνεσθαι ὅτι αὐτόχθονες εἶεν (οἱ ᾿Αθηναῖοι, ὡς καὶ οἱ τέττιγες) [similarly in Su.].

R has τοὺς τέττιγας παρέλαβεν [Su. : -ov R] ἐπειδὴ οἱ παλαιοί κτλ. : the feast Διπολίεια (Pax 420) was held on the 14th of Scirophorion (June-July) and was sacred to Zeus Πολιεύς, ἀπὸ τῶν περὶ τοὺς πελάνους [τῶν -wv MS.] kal τοὺς βοῦς συμβάντων (schol. Pax l.c.). The

festival had two parts, of which the first was connected with the harvests, the second being associated with blood. The latter feature modified the character of the celebration, transforming an essentially joyous feast into one of death. Originally the festival was held at the foot of the citadel, where it was usual to place some corn on the altar of Zeus Hypatus. When blood was introduced, a second altar was required. In classical times, the sacrifice took place on the Acropolis, where one altar was placed near the Erechtheum, on which the fruits of the earth were offered ; the other altar was that of Zeus Polieus. The sacred ox was allowed to devour the fruits, and, in consequence of this sacrilege, was sacrificed on the second altar (985 n.); see Band, De Diipol. sacro Athen., J. Wackernagel, Rh. Mus. xlv. pp. 480 sqq., E. Pottier in Daremberg ef Saglio, Dict. ii. p. 269, Jane Harrison, Proleg. pp- 111 sqq., J. G. Frazer, Golden Bough,? ii. p. 295, Paus. i. 24 § 8,

## 28
§ 11. For the correct form of the word (Διπολίεια) cp. J. Wackernagel, ib. p. 481. ΔΙ sems to have been originally a dialectal dat. of Ζεύς, the word being formed from ἡ θυσία «τῷ» Al «τῷ» πολιεῖ (ep. Δισωτήρια).

τεττίγων : cp. Vesp. 1267 n., Neil on Eq. 1331. Until the time of Pericles, old-fashioned Athenians wore their hair in a κρωβύλος, fastened with an ἔνερσις (Zq. 1331, Thue. i. 6, Heracl. Pont. ap. Athen. 512 ¢, ib. 518 E, 525 F, Lucian, Nav. § 3), in view of their autochthonous origin (for which Antisthenes, fr. 129 Mullach, said they were no better than ἀττέλεβοι). The cicalas seem to have been a kind of spiral passed through the hair. Many of these have been found in Italian tombs (for a representation of which cp. Studniczka in Classen’s Thuc.t i. p. 339, Helbig, Hom. Ep. p. 169 n. 11).

ἀνάμεστα : poetical, cp. H. Richards, Aristoph. ete. Ὁ. 154; here in a bad sense, like ἀνάπλεως (Zccl. 1072), cp. [Dem.] xxv. § 32.

## 985
Schol. V (inf) Κηκίδης [perhaps Kv-: Knkeldns R] διθυράμβων ποιητὴς πάνυ ἀρχαῖος" μέμνηται δὲ αὐτοῦ Κρατῖνος «Πανόπταις R [=1i. p. 61 K. ; ii. p. 106 M.D. || id. (int. ) (ἑορτὴ ἔτι παρὰ ᾿Αθηναίοις ἐπιτελουμένη τῇ ᾿Αθηνᾷʼ ἄλλως.) ἑορτὴ ᾿Αθηναίων πάνυ ἀρχαία: ἐν γὰρ τοῖς Δι πολείοις [R: -λι- V] φασὶ [φησὶ MS. ] βοῦν τὸ πόπανον καταφαγεῖν τὸ παρεσκευασμένον εἰς τὴν θυσίαν καὶ τούτου χάριν βοῦν θύουσιν [also in Su., with much more]. || ἐν τοῖς Διϊπολείοις, διὰ Tov βοῦν τὸν “φαγόντα τὸ πόπανον, καὶ τυθέντα διὰ τοῦ ποπάνου R).

Κηδείδου : cp. 967 n.

Βουφονίων : the antiquated ceremony of slaying the ox, which took place at the Dipolia (984 n.). Possibly the first feast of the B. was originated by an accident, which determined the nature of the expiatory ceremony ; viz. the ox was slain in a fit of passion, for the sacrilegious eating of the meal ; the man (βουτύπος, βουφόνος) who slew the ox, being seized with horror, threw away his axe and fled ; the ox was carved by the δαιτροί, cooked on the second altar, and was then eaten; but, in order to repair the deed, its skin was stuffed, and set on its legs, as if the beast were still alive. But the pretence did not amount to much, as each of the subordinate agents (animate and inanimate) was

accused in turn of the sacrilege (before the Archon sitting in the Prytaneum). Finally, the axe was found guilty and thrown into the sea; see Aelian, VH. viii. 8 3, Topffer, 47. Geneal. p. 149, Stengel in Pauly-Wissowa, Znc. iii. p.

## 105
b, id. Hermes xxviii. p. 489, E. Pottier in Daremberg et Saglio, Dict. ii. p. 270, Mommsen, Heort. pp. 450 sqq., Wilamowitz, Eur. Herc. i. p. 60, Vv. Prott, Rh. Mus. lii. ῬΡ. 187 $qq., Stengel, Opferbrauche d. Gr. p. 217. For representations of the βουφόνος (from a liturgical calendar found at Athens) cp. Daremberg et Saglio, l.c. fig. 2453. Jane Harrison, Proleg. pp. 111 sqq., finds the origin of the festival in man’s demand for flesh food, which, on account of the ἄγος involved, could be procured only by a series of expiations.

ἀλλʼ οὖν: cp. Vesp. 1129 τ.

ταῦτα... ἐκεῖνα : cp. Ach. 41 n.

## 986
**ἄνδρας**: in a complimentary sense, cp. Ach. 707 n.

Μαραθωνομάχους : cp. Ach. 181 n.

παίδευσις : not the same as παιδεία, but emphasizing the activity of the teachers. The word is often mistranslated, e.g. in Zhesm. 175 ζηλῶ σε τῆς παιδεύσεως (‘I envy you for the way in which you have trained yourself’), Thue.

## 11
41 § 1 λέγω τὴν πᾶσαν πόλιν τῆς Ἑλλάδος παίδευσιν εἷναι (Ξε παιδεύτριαν), Plato, Prot. 349 A σεαυτὸν (viz. Protagoras) ἀπέφηνας παιδεύσεως καὶ ἀρετῆς διδάσκαλον ‘a teacher of pedagogy’ (to use ‘a vile phrase’).

## 987
**εὐθύς** : viz. even from childhood.

ἱματίοις : in better days, the boys were γυμνοί, they now are smothered in the ample folds of the cloak, which, according to this passage, was recently introduced ; but the vases tell a different tale, cp. 965 n.

ἐντετυλίχθαι: a comic verb, cp. 10 n., Plut. 692, Diocl. i. p. 769 K. (ii. p.

## 840
M.), Lys. 663 ἐντεθριῶσθαι. From τύλη ‘a bolster,” an Ionic word (Ach.

## 860
n., Phryn. p. 256 Ruth., p. 173 Lobeck, Poll. vii. § 191).

## 988
**ἀπάγχεσθαι**: cp. 1036 n., 1376, Ach. 125 n., Vesp. 686 n.

ὀρχεῖσθαι : schol. V (int) ὠρχοῦντο [γὰρ ἐν R] τοῖς Παναθηναίοις ἐν ὅπλοις ol παῖδες" διὰ «δὲ R> τὸ ἀπρεπές, φησί, προέχοντες τῶν αἰδοίων τὴν ἀσπίδα. id. (int.) (τὸ ἑξῆς τὴν ἀσπίδα τῆς Τριτογενείας ὡς [καὶ MS.] ἐν τῇ πομπῇ ἀσπιδηφορούντων αὐτῶν, δέον περικαλύπτεσθαι τὴν αἰσχύνην): viz. in the πυρρίχη (δοϊϊίογορω  saltatio), borrowed from Sparta, where it was a national dance, in which even women took part (Xen. Anab. vi. 1 88 12 sq., Plato, Legg. 796 B sq., Athen. 630 π, Grasberger, Erzich. etc. iii. p. 298), but at Athens it was reserved for certain festivals. For a description of its merits see Plato, ib.

## 815
A. There is some doubt as to the exact character of the violation of etiquette here ; perhaps the stripling’s left arm was tired from holding the shield, and he was forced to clasp it in front of him, with both hands, against his chest. This was a difficult operation, possible only to a Sir Boyle Roche, if he had to accompany himself with a trumpet while he danced (P. Girard, Educ. athén. p. 216), or if he had to carry a sword, as many hold (but see the extant reliefs, and W. E. Downes, Cl. Rev. xviii. pp. 101 a sqq.) ; possibly (as van Leeuwen suggests), the

modern youth, being unused to light attire (the dancers were ‘naked’), tries to shelter his bare limbs behind his shield. Very similar are the comic complaint of Dionysus in Ran. 1089 sqq., and the picture of a suffering ‘Falstaff’ during a march in Plato, Rep. 556 D. There were prizes for the dance open to men, boys, and children (Mommsen, Heort. pp. 162 sqq., Grasberger, Erzieh. ete. p. 329). For representations on vases cp. JHS. 1884, pl. 43.

Παναθηναίοις : cp. 386 n.

δέον αὐτούς : schol. R (not in V) ὅταν χρεία.

## 989
**προέχων** : cp. 973 n.

ἀμελῇ : for the change to the sing. cp. Vesp. 553 n.

Τριτογενείης : an epic name (72. iv.

## 515
ete., Herod. iv. 188 sq., vii. 141), about whose origin there has been much controversy both in ancient and modern times, see Jane Harrison, Prim. Ath. Ῥ. 64, Gruppe, Myth. p. 250 n. 8, Farnell, Cults etc. i. p. 267, Neil on Eg. 1189. According to Gruppe, l.c., the most probable origin is τριτὼ τρόμος (Hesych. ), so that the name would mean ‘causing fright’; but Aristophanes was not a scientific etymologist, and certainly connected the title with ‘ water’ (Lys. 347, perhaps from Tritonis in Libya, ¢ whence the Greeks obtained the dress and aegis of the statues of Athena,’ as Jane Harrison argues).

The ‘disregard of T.” was shown by their effeminacy in a dance celebrated in honour of the warlike goddess (cp. F. Thiersch, Aristophanea, p. 676).

## 990
**πρὸς ταῦτα**: cp. Pesp. 648 n. Neil (Eg. 622) attempts to distinguish πρὸς ταῦτα from πρὸς τάδε (1030 ete.) as being defiant, while the latter is used in a friendly appeal. The present passage makes the distinction very doubtful.

## 991
Schol. R (not in V) ἀντὶ τοῦ μαθήσῃ μισεῖν τὴν ἐκκλησίαν.

μισεῖν ἀγοράν : the absence of the article (except after a local prep., Vesp.

## 492
n.) is curious, unless the metre justifies it. For the moral objection to frequenting the market-place cp.

## 1003
Eq. 1373, Plato, Theaet. 173 Ὁ (it is strange to philosophers, even in their dreams); hence ἀγοραῖος (Ran. 1015) came to mean ‘a loafer,” and ἀγοράζειν (Ach. 720 n.) ‘to loaf.’

βαλανείων : the objection to *hothouses’ (Shak. Meas. 11. i. 67) was Socratic (Plato, Symp. 1744), and ‘Cynical’ (837 n., Xen. Mem. iii. 13 § 3, Joel, Der echte Sokr. ete. ii. p. 819), but they were popular with the medical profession, except in the case of those enjoying rude health (Plut. De san. praec. § 17). Such asceticism was often ridiculed by the comic poets (Hermipp. i. p. 248 K.; ii. p. 413 M., Com. adesp. iii. Ὁ. 410 K.; iv. p. 661 M.). It is curious that here the Just Reason is arguing in favour of a Socratic practice (Brentano, Untersuch. p. 62).

## 992
**φλέγεσθαι** : schol. V (interlin.)

ἐρυθριᾶν ; or possibly ‘to blaze up,’ cp. Soph. OC. 1695 (a mark of grief). To bear patiently an unseemly jest, or a reproof, in the view of the Greeks, argued a poor spirit ; but it was neither ἀγεννές nor ἀπαίδευτον to smile at a jest «μὴΣ αἰσχύνῃ φλεγόμενον τὴν ψυχήν—even at one’s own expense—that had no ὕβρις in it (Plut. De aud. § 16), since a good man should feel shame only at what is really shameful (id. Cato min. 6).

## 993
Schol. V (int.) (ἀττικῶς ἀντὶ τοῦ θώκων " λέγει δὲ ὅτι τοὺς πρεσβυτέρους προτιμᾶν (μαθήσῃ; καὶ τὸ μὴ) ἀπαίδευτα ποιεῖν εἰς [παρὰ R] τοὺς σαυτοῦ γονεῖς [-ἐας R] [also Su. s.v. ἄχρηστα] ; probably merely a lament for the diminution of the respect paid to age such as has been common in all ages, cp. Juv. xiil. ὅδ. These marks of reverence are mentioned by Herod. (ii. 80) as being peculiar to the Egyptians and Spartans (Tyrtaeus 12. 41 B.%); according to Joél, they were ‘Cynical’ (Xen. Cyr. viii. 7 § 10, where Cyrus is really a Cynic).

**θάκων** : where men met for gossip (=MNéoxat), cp. Hom. Od. xv. 468, Herod. ix. 94, Xen. Lac. pol. 9 § 5, Cyr. viii.

## 7
§ 10, Symp. 4 ὃ 31; it is an old word used in a special sense (Vesp. 186 n.) in comedy (Ran. 1515, 1522), as well as in prose (Plato, Politic. 388 A, Rep.

## 516
E, etc.).

## 994
**περί**: cp. Eg. 831 μιαρώτατος ὧν περὶ τὸν δῆμον, Men. Zau. 59 (of a son) kal περὶ ἔμʼ’ ὡς ἔνεστιν εὐσεβεστάτῳ. Chiefly found in the orators, cp. Lysias xiii. § 2, Isocr. iv. 88 108, 147, 155, vii. § 49 περὶ τοὺς γονέας ἐξαμαρτεῖν.

σκαιουργεῖν: schol. V (interlin.) (πανουργεῖν) ; only here, but σκαιός is a suitable word in the case of an offence against good manners (not against morals, as the scholiast says), cp. 629 n., Vesp. 1183 n.

## 995
Schol. V (int.) (ὅπερ μέλλει τῆς αἰδοῦς τὰ ἀγάλματα πληρῶσαι [also Su. 5.0. ἄχρηστα] τὸ δὲ ἀναπλήσειν ἀντὶ τοῖν) ἀφανίζειν [ἀφανίσειν R], see crit. n.

Αἰδοῦς : the personification of Aids is Spartan, and ‘Cynical’ (Joél), cp. Xen. Symp. 8 § 36 (the Spartans) θεὰν οὐ τὴν ᾿Αναίδειαν ἀλλὰ τὴν Αἰδῶ νομίζουσι : see also Aesch. Sept. 409 τὸν Αἰσχύνης θρόνον | τιμῶντα, Bum. 539.

τἄγαλμα, ‘the ideal’ (of modesty), but ἄ. is more concrete, cp. Vesp. 315 n., Wilam. Herc. 49, Ruhnken ad 77m. p- 13 n. Ὁ Valpy, Plato, Symp. 222 A (Alcibiades says) διοιγομένους δὲ ἰδὼν <the words of Socrates) εὑρήσει θειοτάτους, kal πλεῖστʼ ἀγάλματα ἀρετῆς ἐν αὐτοῖς ἔχοντας (probably a reminiscence of this passage), [Dem.] xxv. § 385 δίκης καὶ εὐνομίας kal αἰδοῦς εἰσι πᾶσιν ἀνθρώποις βωμοί, οἱ μὲν κάλλιστοι καὶ ἁγιώτατοι ἐν αὐτῇ τῇ ψυχῇ ἑκάστου καὶ τῇ φύσει, οἱ δὲ καὶ κοινῇ τοῖς πᾶσι τιμᾶν ἱδρυμένοι, Eur. Hel, 1002 ἔνεστι δʼ ἱερὸν τῆς δίκης ἐμοὶ μέγα | ἐν τῇ φύσει: tmago honesti in Tac. Ann. xvi. 32 § 3 is less concrete than the Greek, and more closely approaches to the English ‘ideal.’

From this passage Aristides (i. p. 77 Dind.) speaks of a young man being an ἄγαλμα τῆς Αἰδοῦς ; his metaphor is ἃ natural extension of the idea here, and does not prove that he found ἀναπλάττειν in his text.

ἀναπλήσειν, ‘to meal,” ‘to slubber,’ ‘to colly,’ ep. 1023, Ach. 847 n., Aesch. Jr. 275 N.2 ἐρῳδιὸς yap ὕψοθεν ποτώμενος | ὄνθῳ σε πλῆσει [codd. -ξει] νηδύος κενώμασιν. [Nauck for χειλ.], Livy iv. 30 § 9 urbs deinde impletur ; generally followed by a gen., but there are exceptions, cp. Thue. ii. 51 8 4 ἕτερος ἀφʼ ἑτέρου θεραmelas ἀναπιμπλάμενοι {τῆς νόσου» ; here the missing gen. can readily be supplied from αἰσχρόν. :

## 997
**μήλῳ βληθείς** : schol. V (int.) ἀντὶ τοῦ ἔρωτι" οὕτως (yap) ἔλεγον ol παλαιοὶ τὸ πτοῆσαι καὶ εἰς ἔρωτα ἀγαγεῖν μήλῳ βάλλειν [similarly in Su. 580.υυ.7ʼ (τὸ δὲ ἀποθραυσθῇς ἀντὶ τοῦ) ἐκπέσῃς [also Su. s.v. ἄχρηστα]. A familiar declaration of love (Diogen. iii. 63), as the tree was sacred to Aphrodite (cp. Theocr. v. 88, vi. 6 sq., Virg. Fel. iii. 64, Catull. lxv.

where see Ellis), and the pips were a symbol of fecundity. There has been a controversy as to what fruit was intended by μῆλον ; it was probably the ‘quince’ (cp. 978 n., Stesich. 29 B.4, Plut. Coni. praec. § 1, Anth. Pal. v.

## 291
Willems, Bull. de I’ Acad. roy. de Belg., 1906, pp. 672 sqq.). Elsewhere it is used of every kind of tree-fruit except the nut; see B. O. Foster, ‘The Symbol of the Apple in Classical Antiquity,” Harvard Stud. x. pp. 39 sqq.

πορνιδίου, puzzel,’ ‘punk,’ ‘drab,’ ‘cot-quean,’ ‘harlotry,” ‘flirt-gill,” ¢ callet,’ “stale,” ἱροῦ wench.’

ἀποθραυσθῇς: a curious metaphor, possibly from chariot-driving (Pind. O. vi. 97), but more probably from shipwreck. Much difficulty has been caused by the gen.: ‘be broken off from your fair fame’ (L. & S.) is not a happy rendering ; ‘make shipwreck of your fair fame’ is satisfactory in sense, but does not explain the gen. ἀποθραύειν is generally used of breaking off a fragment from a larger body (Aesch. Pers.

## 410
ἀποθραύει πάντα Φοινίσσης | νεὼς κόρυμβα); here a man’s good name is the larger body from which the man himself is detached. According to Blaydes, the metaphor is from horns etc. (Plato, Phaedr. 248 B οὗ δὴ. πολλαὶ πολλὰ πτερὰ θραύονται).

## 998
**ἀντειπεῖν** : to contradict an old man — much more one’s father — was contrary to good taste, cp. Isocr. viii. § 49.

Ἰαπετόν, ‘ass unpolicied’ (Shak. Ani. v. ii. 319), ‘a decayed dotant’ (Cor. Vv. ii. 47); schol. V (ext.) λῆρον, μωρόν" (ὁ δὲ ᾿Ιαπετὸς εἷς τῶν Τιτάνων) [also Su.]; cp. Vesp. 1480 n.

καλέσαντα μνησικακῆσαι: in accordance with the Greek idiom (cp. Ach.

## 202
n., Vesp. 577 n.), the main idea is in the particip., ‘spitefully to call his age Iapetus.” For the aor. particip. cp. Vesp. 535 crit. app.

## 999
**μνησικακῆσαι**: according to many commentators, governing τὴν 7., but an acc. (except of a neut. pron.) is unexampled after this verb. μνησικακεῖν is, in general, a grave word, with a familiar political sense, cp. Plu. 1146 μὴ μνησικακήσῃς εἰ σὺ Φυλὴν κατέλαβες (a proverbial use, since the sense, as given by schol. R, is εἰ καὶ ἐπλούτησας, μὴ ἐπαρθῇς), an allusion to the famous amnesty, after the return of Thrasybulus (for the terms of which cp. Andoc. Myst. § 90, J. M. Stahl, Rh. Mus. xlviii. ῬΡ. 250 sqq.); see H. Weber, Aristoph. Stud. p. 177, Romer, Sitzungsb. p. 235.

ἡλικίαν : schol. V (ext.) (τὴν ἡλικίαν) τοῦ πατρός, (δηλονότι" τὸ δὲ ἐνεοττοτροφήθης, ἀντὶ τοῦ ἐτράφη) [also Su. 5.ν. ἄχρηστα]; in a general sense, which is neither ‘youth’ nor ‘old age.” τὴν ἡ. is more pathetic than τὸν γέροντα, which might have been expected, the sense being ‘the years that have brought him from youth to old age were spent in rearing you.” Possibly, in the choice of the word, the poet was influenced by a reminiscence of Hom. Od. iii. 49 ἀλλὰ νεώτερός ἐστιν, ὁμηλικίη δʼ ἐμοὶ αὐτῷ. It is possible to take τὴν 1%. with ’Idmr., ‘Iapetus in respect of his age’ (cp. Herod. iii. 134 νέος ἡλικίην). This construction would be more in accordance with the prose usage, but the pathos would thereby, perhaps, be lessened.

ἐξ: possibly, as Professor Beare sug-

Q gests to me, ‘out of which,’ τὴν 7. being treated as a kind of φόν, out of which Phidippides was bred, as (in 4wves 699) the φὸν ὑπηνέμιον, laid by Eros, ἐνεόττευσεν γένος ἡμέτερον (viz. of the birds). Thus, the reproach ‘Iapetus’ would be converted into the honourable title of the first son of Earth, and one of the fathers of the race. Not equivalent to ὑπό, unless he is speaking in tragic phrase (Aesch. Ag. 735 ἐκ θεοῦ προσεθρέφθη, Sobol. Praep. p. 85).

ἐνεοττοτροφήθης : cp. 334 n.

Su.] τινες, | βληχητὰ τέκνα, καὶ οὐδαμῶς τοὐφμοῦς τρόπου)" τὰ δὲ ὀνόματα αὐτῶν ἸΤελέσιππος, Δημοφῶν, Περικλῆς [also 8..7" (τὸ δὲ Βλιτομάμμαν ἀντὶ τοῦ μωρόν. καὶ γὰρ τὸ βλίτον μωρὸν εἶναι δοκεῖ λάχανον) [similarly in Su.]. Hippocrates, the son of Ariphron, and nephew

of Pericles, was general in 426-5 B.C., and 424-3 B.C., and was Demosthenes’ colleague in besieging Nicaea (Thuc. iv.

sq.) ; he fell at Delium in the following winter (ib. 89 sqq.), see Bergk, Rell. com. Att. p. 350, Kirchner, Prosop. Att. i. p. 502. His sons were swinish, and stupid ; and also of a litigious temperament, as, on reaching man’s estate, they prosecuted their guardian, who delivered, in his own defence, a speech written by Lysias (fr. 122 Didot, Blass, Beredsamk.*

(Ach. Introd. p. lix.), Athen. 96 E ; it has a ‘Cynic’ flavour, cp. Plut. De cupid. div. § 7 (Diogenes jestingly said) Μεγαρέως «ἂν» ἀνδρὸς βέλτιον εἶναι κριὸν ἢ ὑὸν γενέσθαι (viz. ὗν, in reference to Ach. 738 sqq.).

εἴξεις : cp. 341 n.

βλιτομάμμαν: an epithet of the cousins of Hippocrates, cp. schol. Plato, I. Ale. 118 E Ξάνθιππος καὶ Πάραλος, οἱ Περικλέους υἱοί, ods καὶ βλ. ἐκάλουν. βλίτ[τ]αι γὰρ καὶ βλίτ[ τ]ωνες (Ach. Introd. Ῥ. 111. (5)) οἱ εὐήθεις" μάμματα (a word unknown elsewhere) δὲ τὰ βρώματα " καὶ τὸ ἐσθίειν ᾿Αργεῖοι [αἱ μαῖαι Kock] μαμμιᾶν (also in Phot.) ἔλεγον: ἐκ τούτων οὖν σύνθετον τὸ βλ. ὁ ἐσθίων εὐήθως, ὡς καὶ συκομάμμας ὁ cukopdyos: so Hesych. ἔστι [Naber for ἐπὶ] τῆς παιδικῆς φωνῆς ἐσθίειν. On the other hand, according to Phrynichus (ap. Bekk. 4n. p. 31. 3) βλίτον (=‘orach’) was an insipid vegetable (in Men. iii. p. 245 K. ; iv. p. 307 M. βλιτάδες yvvaikes=‘ worthless’), and μάμμα was ‘mother’; hence the compound would mean ὁ ἐπὶ τῇ τῆς μητρὸς τροφῇ ἐξίτηλος γενόμενος. The Romans followed this explanation, cp. Plaut. Truc. 854 blitea et luteast meretrixz nisi quae sapit in wino ad rem suom (possibly, as v. Leeuwen suggests, a translation of Menander’s phrase). But Bergk may be right in interpreting βλιτο- as μέλι (cp. μελιττίδης ‘noodle,’ ‘John-a-dreams,’ Vesp. 336 n., Ran. 991), in which case the compound should be compared with τηθαλλαδοῦς (Com. adesp. iii. p. 400 K.; not in M.), μαμμάθρεππος, or μαμμάκυθος (Ran. 990, from μαμμία, the cry of a child).

Lac. pol. 5 8 8 ἀπὸ τῶν αὐτῶν σίτων ol μὲν διαπονούμενοι εὔχροοί Te kal εὔσαρκοι καὶ εὔρωστοί εἰσιν, οἱ δʼ’ ἄπονοι πεφυσημένοι τε καὶ αἰσχροὶ καὶ ἀσθενεῖς ἀναφαίψονται, but it was not left to the Cynics to discover this.

IIL. ii. 60 ‘ he ambled up and down With shallow jesters, and rash bavin (= ‘brushwood’) wits, Soon kindled and soon burnt,” ‘giving more light than heat.’ Their language was ‘linsey-woolsey’ (Alls well 1v. i. 13), ‘flip-flap’ (Dekker, Shoemaker’s Holiday, 111. v.), ‘rimbleramble’ (Urquhart’s Rabelais),  skimbleskamble stuff’ (1 Hen. IV 111. i. 153), ‘tiddle-taddle, or pibble-pabble’ (Hen. Viv. i. 71). For τρίβολος cp. Lys.

## 576
Virg. Georg. i. 153; for ἐκτράπελος ‘ devious,” ‘strange,’ ¢ monstrous,’ cp. Pherecr. i. p. 188 K. (ii. p. 327 M.) (of the art of Timotheus) ἐκτράπελους μυρμηκιάς, Epicharm. 67 K. ἐκτραπελογάστρους ὄνους (‘of intolerable entrails,’ Wives v. v. 165), Ael. HA. xiv. § 9 (of the sea-lion) ; hence the compound seems to connote ‘sterile preciosities.’

## 1004
**ἑλκόμενος** : cp. Fg. 710. According to some commentators, mid. (like λοιδορεῖσθαι 62 n.), ‘dragging one another,’ but it is simpler to take it passively.

γλισχραντιλογεξεπιτρίπτου : schol. V (ext.) ἐκ Tov γλίσχρος [-ov MS.] καὶ ἀντιλογίαν ἔχειν [-οντος MS.] kal ἐπίτριπτος [-ov MS.] σύγκειται: οἷον τοῦ δυναμένου ἐπιτρῖψαι [also Su.], apparently taking -e£- as the fut. of ἔχειν (cp. προδωσέταιρος, τρυσάνωρ) ; but it is more probably a part of a compound ἐξεπιτρίβειν, which does not occur, but may be allowed in such a jest κατὰ παρωνυμίαν (μον. Introd. p- li.). γλίσχρος (originally ‘viscous,’ cp. Hippocr. Περὶ τ. ἐν κεφ. 7p. 111. p. 236 L.) seems to have been associated with the ‘minute philosophers’ (Ack. 452 n.) ; γλισχρολογία, ¢straw-splitting,’ occurs in Diog. L. ii. § 30 (of Socrates).

The Gargantuan compound seems to mean ‘a bagatelle of a case, which, after infinite straw-splitting, ends only in your undoing.’

§ 1 (with Frazer's notes). It lay near Colonus Hippius, a little north of Lenormant’s well-known monument ; cp. Milton, PR. iv. 244 sqq. ‘The olive grove of Academe, Plato’s retirement, where the Attic bird Trills his thickwarbl’d notes the summer long.’

ὑπό : rare c. dat., cp. Pesp. 108, 386, Thesm. 529, 562, Eccl. 87; generally of what is actually beneath another object (as of wax under the nails, Vesp. 108), cp. Sobol. Praep. p. 221.

poplars : cp. Frazer on Paus. i. 30 § 1. The olive-trees were originally twelve in number, and were believed to be the offshoots of the first olive-tree planted on the Acropolis (Soph. OC. 698 sqq.). The Academe was first planted, and its εὔσκιοι δρόμοι (Eupol. i. p. 265; ii. p.

M.) laid out, by Cimon (Plut. Cim.

§ 11).

and H. Richards, Aristoph. etc. p. 159, Schnee, De codd. p. 42. ἀποθρέξῃ of R etc. has been defended by reference to Plato, Rep. 613 C οὐχ oi μὲν δεινοί Te καὶ ἄδικοι δρῶσιν ὅπερ oi δρομῆς, ὅσοι ἂν θέωσιν εὖ ἀπὸ τῶν κάτω, ἀπὸ δὲ τῶν ἄνω μή; τὸ μὲν πρῶτον ὀξέως ἀποπηδῶσι, τελευτῶντες δὲ καταγέλαστοι γίγνονται, τὰ ὦτα ἐπὶ τῶν ὥμων ἔχοντες καὶ ἀστεφάνωτοι ἀποτρέχοντες (but this means ‘running home’ as in Xen. Oec. 11 § 18).

## 1007
**μίλακος** : perhaps ‘bryony,’ cp. Aves 216, Eur. Bacch. 108 (with Sandys’ note), Theophr. HP. iii. 18 § 1, Aelian, VH. iii. § 1; schol. V (int.) (εἶδος βοτάνης [also Su.]* ὁμοίως καὶ ἡ) ἀπραγμοσύνη εἶδος ἄνθους" || οἷον πάσης εὐωδίας ὄζων καὶ ἀσφαλείας. || ἢ ἀπραγμοσύνη ἀντὶ τοῦ («οὐ R> πολυπράγμων dv <Ruth.: ἢν ΜΗ.» [similarly in Su. s.v. ἀπραγ., who adds οἱ δὲ ὅτι a. φυτὸν ἐν A. φυόμενον] :; in a portion of this note the scholiast (said by another scholiast to be Aristophanes the Grammarian, but he probably misunderstood his comment; cp. A. Nauck, 4». Byz. frr. pp. 214 sq. ; also in Hesych. s.v.) takes the πυραμοῦς for sheer idiocy.

ὄζων : cp. Vesp. 38 n., 1059 n., Xen. Symp. 2 § 4.

ἀπραγμοσύνης : a surprise. It is Aristophanes’ way to mingle incongruous images (Ach. Introd. pp. Ixxii. sqq.), e.g. Vesp. 710, Pax 595 τοῖς ἀγροίκοισιν “γὰρ ἦσθα χῖδρα καὶ σωτηρία. For a. cp. Vesp. 1040 n.

λεύκης, ‘silver poplar,” sacred to Heracles (Virg. Ποῖ. 7. 61 populus Alcidae gratissima), and worn by young athletes devoted to his service (as by Delphis in Theocr. ii. 121, cp. also Ov. Her. ix. 64, Hor. 0d. i. 7. 23). For the origin of its connexion with Heracles cp. Paus. v.

§ 3.

Od. iii. 18. 14), an impossible sense here, unless φύλλα means the ‘ catkins,” which fall in the early spring, and cover the ground with a light covering which has a resinous odour (Willems, Bull. de UAdcad. roy. de Belg., 1906, p. 680).

πλάτανος : the plane-trees in the Academe were famous in later times (Pliny, NH. xii. 5). In tropical countries. they were prized for their shade (ib. xvii.

## 12
§ 18, Plato, Phaedr. 230 B). Though, at one time, common in Athens, where they were planted even in the Agora, they soon ceased to exist ; so that Cicero (De orat. 1 § 28) suggests that the tree by the Ilissus was a product of Plato’s imagination.

Ψψιθυρίζῃ : like a lover with his ὀαριστύς (Theocr. xxvii. 67); cp. Milton, PR. ii.

## 26
‘where winds with reeds and osiers whispering play.” The same metaphor is more prosaically employed in Eg. 806 καὶ στεμφύλῳ εἰς λόγον ἔλθῃ (‘tackle’).

## 1010
**πρός**, τούτόις : schol. R (not in V) οἷς λέγω, a jocose anticlimax, which is possible in a Pnigus. Note that πρός is an adverb here (cp. Lys. 628 καὶ διαλλάττειν πρὸς ἡμᾶς ἀνδράσιν Λακωνικοῖς, Ran. 611 κλέπτοντα πρὸς τἀλλότρια, ib. 697 πρὸς δὲ τούτοις εἰκὸς ὑμᾶς κτλ.), and not a preposition, since the classical constr. of προσέχειν is τὸν νοῦν τινι (varied by πρός τινι τὸν ν. ἔχειν in Plato, Prot. 324 A, but in Polyb. xv. 26 § 8 τὸν νοῦν πρὸς οὐδενὶ προσεῖχον τῶν λεγομένων, quoted by Iltz, Praep. p. 67 ; see Neil on Eq. 815).

## 1011
The enumeration here resembles, in expression, the catalogue of the points of Adonis’ horse in Shak. Venus ete. 293 sqq. ‘Round-hoof’d, short-jointed, fetlocks shag and long, Broad breast, full eye, small head, and nostril wide, High crest, short ears, straight legs, and passing strong, Thin mane, thick tale, broad buttock, tender hide.’

**λιπαρόν** : hardly so, as he will be taught to avoid ‘hot-houses’ (991). The epithet is perhaps not very happy, as it is opposed to <or7fos> λεπτόν (1017).

## 1012
**λαμπράν** : λευκήν of RV ete. is properly used of the parts of the body not covered by hair, or clothed ; here of the brilliant colour of health, )( ὠχράν 1017. It would not be a happy epithet in this context, as it is associated with women (Keel. 387, Chaeremon, 7’G'F. p. 786 N.2 ἔκειτο δʼ ἡ μὲν λευκὸν els σεληνόφως | φαίνουσα μαστὸν λελυμένης ἐπωμίδος © the argent of her breast,” or perhaps ‘ bare’) or effeminate men (Sosicr. iil. p. 391 K.; iv. p. 591 M. ὅταν γάρ, οἶμαι, λευκὸς ἄνθρωπος παχύς, | ἀργός, λάβῃ δίκελλαν εἰωθὼς τρυφᾶν | πενταστάτηρον, γίγνεται τὸ πνεῦμʼ ἄνω, Ἐπ. Bacch. 457).

## 1034
8q. An Anticataceleusmus, but not introduced as usually, by ἀλλά.

The iambic tetrameter sounds the note of the following speech, which is full of wordy altercation and ignoble arguments. Aristophanes generally clothes such scenes in this metre (e.g. in the base discussions in the Knights between the Paphlagonian and the Sausage-seller), while the nobler debates are in anapaestic tetrameters. It is interesting to contrast Euripides’ and Aeschylus’ speeches in Ran. 905 sqq. (See Vesp. Introd. pp. xli. sq.)

**δεινῶν** : schol. V (ext.) ἀντὶ τοῦ μεγάλων.

**εἴπερ** c. fut. ; cp. 443 n., 7768).

## 1056
This class of unhistorical argument, based on passages taken from holy writ, has been familiar in all ages. It was particularly frequent at Athens, where the old poets were universally regarded as the educators par excellence, and the depositaries of all knowledge. The Sophists were very familiar with the Homeric poems, and Protagoras set great store by his ability to analyze them (Plato, Prof. 339 A), but even Plato often appeals to them as a final authority (Freeman, Schools ete. pp. 228 sqq., P. Girard, Educ. athén. p. 140). In this case, it suffices to silence the piously-minded Just Reason. The argument is an etymological one based on the supposed identity of ἀγορή and ἀγορά, ἀγορᾶσθαι and ἀγοράζειν.

## 1
Hen. IV 11. iv. 218 ‘spit in my face, call me horse.” Perhaps best represented by ‘embossed rascal’ (=worn out deer, ep. Shak, 1 Hen. IV τῶι. iii. 177) or ‘hobby-horse ’ (Jonson, Cynthia's Revels, v. il.) or ‘hackney’ (Shak. LLL. 111. 33).

## 1071
It is interesting to note that the Unjust Reason is here advocating everything that the Socratic training (in 413 sqq.) aimed at keeping in check ; see Brentano, Untersuch. p. 63. But there is no proof of ‘contamination’ here. The Sophist is all things to all men, and it would be absurd to seek to win the adherence of Phidippides by recommending habits which appealed to his rustic father (in 416 sqq., where see note). Besides, the arguments of the Unjust Reason are merely ad hominem, and are not intended to be serious. In Antid. §§ 285 sq., Isocrates, who was a Sophist, attributes such principles to other Sophists; but he was a jealous man, and it is not well to put too much trust in statements made by the genus irritabile oratorum.

Schol. V (adscr. ext.) λείπει τὰ κακά.

ἀνάγκας, ‘the laws of nature’ (377 n.) which the Unjust Reason, like the libertine in all ages, identifies with his animal passions. So in Plato, Rep.

## 458
» in the common life of the selected class, men and women will come together ὑπʼ ἀνάγκης τῆς ἐμφύτου, which ‘necessity’ is not ‘geometrical’ but ‘sexual,’ and thus much stronger than the other πρὸς τὸ πείθειν τε καὶ ἕλκειν τὸν πολὺν λεών. The phrase ‘necessities of nature’ was a neologism at this time, but the orators, like the modern journalists, circulated it, and it soon became trite, cp. Aeschin. i. § 138, Isocr. iv. § 84, Eur. 7ro. 886 εἴτʼ ἀνάγκη φύσεος, εἴτε νοῦς βροτῶν «Ζεύς».

## 1076
**ἥμαρτες** : a common ‘hypocoristic’ (Ach. Introd. p. liv.) use of ἁμαρτία (like peccare Hor. S. i. 2. 63), especially in Eurip., ep. Suppl. 900, El. 1036, Bacch. 29 ; most famous from [S. John,] Ev. 8. 11 ; so the προκυκλίς in Herodas 1. 61 sq. ἀλλʼ ὦ τέκνον μοι, Μητρίχη, μίαν ταύτην | ἁμαρτίην δός. The Unjust Reason is not defiant, but hypocritical here.

ἠράσθης : from ἔραμαι (Kiithner-Blass, ib. 8 290, H. Richards, Aristoph. ete. p. 152), ἃ poetical word, cp. 1304, Vesp. 751, Pax 1098, Ran. 1022, Theocr. viii. 60, ἐρασθήσομαι Aesch. μην. 852.

ἐμοίχευσάς τι: cp. Ach. 842, Ey. 1242 καί τι kal βινισκόμην, Vesp. 140 n. For the absence of the diaeresis cp. 7768}. Introd. p. xlii.

ἐλήφθης, ‘taken with the manner’ (viz. ‘in the act,’ Shak. Wint. 1v. 111. 755), cp. Com. adesp. iii. p. 398 K. (iv. p. 608 M.), [Dem.] lix. § 65. If the woman was mother, wife, sister, daughter or mistress (ἣν ἂν ἐπ’ ἐλευθέροις παισὶν ἔχῃ), the offended person might exact the penalty of death, without being prosecuted for murder. Such was the decision of the Court of Areopagus, ep. Lysias i. §§ 30 sq., Dem. xxiii. § 55, Plato, Legg. 874 c.

## 1077
**ἀδύνατος** : schol. V (ext.) (ov yap δυνατὸς εἶ λόγοις νικῆσαι αὐτοὺς ἐναντίον). It is curious that, in Plato, Gorg. 486 B, Callicles accuses Socrates of having unfitted his pupils for defending themselves in a court, since εἰλιγγιῶσι kal χασμῶνται, if they are confronted by an accuser πάνυ φαῦλος (possibly an allusion to Socrates’ own fate in the case of Meletus). The Unjust Reason has certainly no trait of the real Socrates ; he is a Sophist pur sang.

**αὐτόν** : viz. the injured husband ; a possible Greek vagueness, where the English journalist might write ‘him (the plaintiff),” cp. Eq. 263, Plut. 503 (αὐτά-- τὸν πλοῦτον), Plato, Rep. 4828 ; but see crit. n.

## 1080
**ἐπανενεγκεῖν** : for the infin. cp. Vesp. 386 n. ; for the verb cp. Solon 11 B.¢ Probably both the verb and the idea are borrowed from Euripides (cp. Ion 827, Or. 76, Bacch. 29), whose heroes and heroines, when reduced ad incitas, are wont eis τὸν θεὸν τὸ φαῦλον ἀναφέρειν (IT. 390).

The appeal to the example of Zeus, who has been dethroned, is an amusing (cp. 905 n.) instance of the devil citing scripture for his purpose. It is sufficient here as an arg. ad hominem (Freeman, Schools ete. p. 230) ; see Appendix.

## 1081
**ἥττων** : schol. R (not in V) ἀντὶ τοῦ ἡττᾶται ; commonly used of one who ‘makes courtesy to’ the tender passion, cp. Soph. Trach. 489, Eur. Andr. 631 ; but not confined to him, cp. Plut. 363 κέρδους ἥττονες, Xen. Mem. 1. 5 § 1 ἄνδρα ἥττω γαστρὸς ἢ οἴνου ἢ ἀφροδισίων ἢ πόνου ἢ ὕπνου.

## 1083
**ῥαφανιδωθῇ** : schol. V (ext.) οὕτως γὰρ τοὺς ἁλόντας [-ὥντας MS.] μοιχοὺς ἤκιζον: ῥαφανῖδας λαμβάνοντες ἔβαλλον εἰς τοὺς πρωκτοὺς αὐτῶν, καὶ παρατίλλοντες αὐτοὺς θερμὴν [Su.: λεπτὴν MS., from 178] τέφραν ἐπέπασσον, βασάνους ἱκανὰς ἐργαζόμενοι [similarly in Su.]. {{(ὅτι καὶ παρετίλλοντο τέφραν αὐτοῖς ἐπιπαττόμενοι [τοις MS.] εἰς τὸν πρωκτόν.) Hence, by a jest, an adulterer’s oath was said to be written els τέφραν, cp. Philonides i. p. 256 K. (ii. p. 423 M.) ὅρκους δὲ μοιχῶν εἰς τέφραν ἐγὼ γράφω. As can readily be imagined, adulterers, even when caught in the act, were rarely put to death by the injured husbands, who preferred either to prosecute the culprits, or to exact a heavy fine, and to subject them to certain humiliating penalties, such as παρατιλμός (ep. Plut.

## 168
**ὁ δʼ ἁλούς γε μοιχὸς διὰ σέ** (Plutus) που παρατίλλεται, viz. ‘gets off with a plucking’ by paying a fine). For ῥαφανίδωσις cp. Lucian, Peregr. ὃ 9, Alciphr. iii. 62 § 4 (=iii. 26 Schepers); a scorpion (Plato Com. i. p. 647 K. ; ii. p. 673 M.) or a mugilis (Catull. 15. 19, with Ellis’

note, Juv. 10, 317) was used for the same purpose. When the culprit was caught, it is said to have been customary to cry ὦ Λακιάδαι (the deme where the best ῥαφανῖδες grew), cp. Leutsch-Schneidewin on App. prov. v. 43.

πιθόμενος : for the absence of the caesura cp. Vesp. Introd. p. xliii.

Schol. V (int.) (ἀντὶ τοῦ ποίαν ἕξει γνώμην μὴ εἶναι εὐρύπρωκτος).

τὸ μή: cp. Aves 36, Ran. 68, Lys.

## 1199
Goodwin, M7. §§ 811 sqq., Kiihner- Gerth, Gram. ὃ 514 A. 9. For the synizesis cp. Ach. 860 n.

εὐρύπρωκτος : a jest kal ὁμωνυμίαν on the literal meaning of the word (‘a loose companion,” Shak. Rich. 11 v. iii. 7), which does not here mean cinaedus, as Blaydes thinks.

## 1085
The change into iambic trimeters is not without point. The Just Reason thinks his last argument conclusive, and it is followed by a dramatic silence, during which the Unjust Reason turns towards the audience and studies their faces. After a thrilling pause, he again confronts his opponent with a triumphant air, and, by means of the change of metre, marks the fact that, in his opinion, the debate is concluded, and after a short struggle his opponent throws up the sponge (Mazon, Comp. ete. p. 59). There is a similar change of metre in Eg. 442, preceded by the explanation τὸ πνεῦμ’ ἔλαττον γί- Ὕνεται. In like manner here, the senarii mark the calm of conscious superiority after the fiery thrust and counter-thrust of the preceding scene.

**τί**: schol. R (not in V) ἀντὶ τοῦ οὐδέν.

## 1086
**τούτου**: schol. R (not in V) τοῦ εἶναι εὐρύπρωκτος.

Schol. V (int.) (λείπει ἡ εἴς, ἵνʼ ἢ [εἰς τοῦτο] R> ἐὰν εἰς τοῦτο νικηθῇς [παρʼ ἐμοῦ [ἐμοὶ R], ὅτι οὐδέν ἐστι κακὸν τὸ εἶναι εὐρύπρωκτος, (τί εἰπῆς ;)

τοῦτο : cogn. acc., cp. 492 π.

νικηθῇς ἐμοῦ : in his hour of victory the Unjust Reason abandons scurrility and talks in tragic phrase, cp. Soph. Aj. 1353, Eur. Med. 315, Kiihner-Gerth, Gram. § 420 A. 8.

## 1089
As Kock* points out, there is a progression here, from the συνἤγοροι, who were mere officials, through the ‘tragedians’ and representatives of the sovereign demus to the demus itself (viz. the spectators). Schol. V (int.) ἀντὶ τοῦ τίνες εἰσὶν οἱ ῥήτορες ;

συνηγοροῦσιν : cp. Vesp. Excursus VII.

## 1090
**εὐρυπρώκτων**, ‘the basa-moncues’ (Dekker, Shoemaker’s Holiday 1. 1) ; a constant insinuation against those who were in training for public life, cp. Ach. 716 n., Eq. 877 sqq., Feel. 112 sq., Plato Com. i. p. 653 K. (ii. p. 681 M.) κεκολλόπευκαςʼ τοιγαροῦν ῥήτωρ ἔσῃ. Doubtless it was a jest. It is developed, with delicious irony, by ‘Aristophanes’ in Plato, Symp. 192 A, where he argues that such men, by virtue of their origin (ib. 191 E) are inspired by θάρρος, ἀνδρεία, and ἀρρενωπία ; and so τελεωθέντες μόνοι ἀποβαίνουσιν eis τὰ πολιτικὰ ἄνδρες οἱ Τοιουτοι.

## 1091

V] παραδοθέντι μανθάνειν τῷ ἑτέρῳ τὸ ἱμάτιον (δὲ) δίδωσιν ἵνα εὐχερῶς αὐτομολήσῃ πρὸς αὐτούς ; see 1112 crit. ἡ. But no weight should be given to this note, which, even if the text were certain, is a stupid production (pace Heidhiis, Uber ἃ. Wolken, p. 11). The humour of the passage would be lost if the speaker were other than the Just Reason.

κινούμενοι : a jest κατʼ ἐξαλλαγὴν φωνῇ “for θεώμενοι, and addressed to them. But Gottling (Uber d. Redoct. ete. p. 28) and Brentano (Untersuch. p. 105) hold that, with these words, he turns towards the Pensoir, and addresses the school-men, who formed a sort of philosophic clique with such characteristics.

## 1104
**δέξασθε**: addressed to the Pensoir and its inmates (Gottling, Uber d. Redact. p- 28). The verb implies a gift (such as Strepsiades made to Socrates in 497);

but the action was probably regarded as symbolical, implying a change of life, and the assumption of a new man (so S. Mark, £v. 10. 50, Hor. O. iii. 16. 22 nil cupientium | nudus castra peto ete.), or possibly an admission of defeat (Brentano, Untersuch. p. 105). Other commentators (with schol.) think that the Just Reason divests himself of his cloak so as to increase his activity, but it required no activity to join the victorious party among the spectators. In matters of humour the scholiasts are usually ὁ“ full dishes of fool,” and should receive scant consideration.

ἐξαυτομολῶ : as in Hor. 7.6., diudtum partes linquere gestio. The verb is rare, and does not occur again until Aen. Tact. 24. For the anap. in an iambic dimeter cp. Zp. 374 (where, however, πρηγορέωνα may be an instance of synizesis), ib. 445, Ran. 984 (in a parody of Euripides).

## 1105-1112
These lines have caused much trouble to the commentators, cp. Weyland, De Ar. Nub. p. 37, Denis, Com. gr.1i. p. 33 n. On the one hand, Gottling (Uber ἃ. Redact. ete. p. 29) and Kochly (4kad. Vort.) think that they were inserted by Aristophanes’ literary executor, Araros, who wished to furnish some connexion between the ‘debate’ and the succeeding scenes. On the other hand, Fritzsche (De fab. ab Ar. retract. i. pp.

## 12
sqq.), Teuffel (Philol. vii. pp. 333 sqq.), and Ritter (Philol. xxxiv. p. 452) argue that their proper place was in the first edition, in which the instruction of Phidippides was probably undertaken by Socrates himself, and not by the Λόγοι. 1 agree with Beer (Zahl d. Schausp. p. 115) in assigning 1105 sq.,

## 1111
to the Unjust Reason. After 877 sqq. it would be absurd for Socrates to ask Strepsiades whether he desired his son to be taught; but the Unjust Reason, who was not present during that scene, might reasonably inquire as to his wishes. Biicheler (A. Jahrb. lxxxiii. p. 672), Textor (Zur dram. Tech. ii. p. 27), and Ritter argue that the passage originally followed a scene in which Phidippides was subjected by Socrates to a vigorous examination. This hypothesis, though unsupported by evidence, is possible,

but it demonstrates nothing as regards the second edition, in which, according to Hypothesis VII., many changes were made ἐν τῇ τῶν προσώπων διαλλαγῇ. Beer’s hypothesis accounts for the absence of a choral ode here, and also provides, during the Second Parabasis, an interval during which the actor, who elsewhere wore the mask of Socrates, had time to change his apparel.

## 1105
**τί δῆτα** ; eh bien! Since these

EA particles are not in place at the beginning of a scene, they imply that the speaker isnot a new arrival, as, according to the usual arrangement, Socrates would be. The Unjust Argument, having defeated his opponent, turns to Strepsiades with an air of triumph, and puts to him a query which is of the nature of a ‘rhetorical question.” After the collapse of the Just Reason, Strepsiades’ reply is not doubtful.

ἀπάγεσθαι: the vox propria of a school-master (Zg. 993) who wishes to get rid of a boy.

## 1112

ἔγωγε: see crit. ἢ. The remark is ἃ reference to Phidippides’ words in 120 ; now that Strepsiades has gained his point, he can afford to laugh at himself, The jest is characteristic of the man (cp. 504); and there is no reason why the line should be assigned to Phidippides. In. deed, there is humour in Strepsiades’ referring, ironically, to Phidippides’ fear (in 120), now that it is pretty clear his instruction will not be of an ascetic type.

## 1113-1130
Second Parabasis.

## 1113
The iambic tetrameter serves as a Commatium to the succeeding Parabasis, and also marks the transition to the Epirrheme. Here the Chorus for the first time (if the Parabasis is excluded), shows its hand. A change of view is usual, on the part of the Chorus, in the early plays of Aristophanes, but in different circumstances. Usually the Chorus takes a vital part in the action ; it represents the dominant majority, whom the poet (in the Agon) aims at convincing of its blindness; to it the two protagonists in the ‘debate’ address their arguments, and it is the Chorus which invariably pronounces the decision. But in the Clouds the Chorus plays the part of passive but malignant onlookers, while infatuated mortals fume and fret before its eyes, and are lured on to their destruction. Such is the admission of the Chorus in 1454 sqq. Here the warning recalls that of Phidippides in 865, and it is to be noted that the best codd. here assign the line to him.

## 1115
8qqg. A scholium (not in RV)

rightly holds these lines to be an incomplete Parabasis ; the tone is certainly ‘ Parabatic,” cp. Aves 1101 sqq., Feel.

## 1155
They are a curious piece of work, thrown here ὁ as if shipwrecked and blown about by the winds’ (Gottling, Uber d. Redact. ete. p. 25). Some writers (Του 8] - Kaehler,® Einl. pp. 39 sqq., Kock,® Einl. §§ 40 sq., Biicheler, WN. Jahrb. 1xxxiii. p. 674) assign the lines, on account of their confidence of victory, to the first edition and this view is probably correct. Gottling (ib. p. 21) suggests that they formed the ἁπλοῦν of the Parabasis in the first edition (now supplanted by the ‘Eupolideans’) ; according to H. Kochly (4kad. Vortr.) they were its Antepirrheme : for other suggested rearrangements see Fritzsche, Qu. Ar. pp. 188 sq., id. De fab. ab Ar. retract. i. pp- 15 sqq., G. Schwandke, De Nub. prior. p. 162. But there is no evidence for any of these hypotheses; and I fail to see here ‘an irrefragable proof’ of a faulty contamination of the two editions of the play. A Parabasis is rarely connected with the plot of the play, and the second Parabasis is nearly always an incomplete piece of work, cp. Heidhiis, Uber d. Wolken, p. 7.

## 1131-1213
Episodium II.

## 1131
As is usual in the plays of Aristophanes, the succeeding scenes develop the practical consequences of the theme of the play. This is done, as in the Acharnes (see 719-970 n.), by a couple of symmetrical scenes, which are a good example of what has been called ‘Doric Comedy’ The first scene, which is divided into two parts, develops the happy consequences of Phidippides’ education ; the second scene (the second Agon) ushers in the tragic dénouement of Strepsiades’ disillusionment (cp. Mazon, Comp. etc. p. 60).

**πέφρικʼ ἐγὼ μέν**, αὐός εἰμι τῷ δέει, Alciphr. ii. 1 § 1 (= iv. 16 Schepers); an epic verb in the sense ‘to bristle’ (in Ran. 822, in a hexam.), but found occasionally in Attic, in the sense ‘to shudder at’ with ‘an agued fear’ (Shak. Cor, 1. iv. 38), cp. Aesch. Sept. 720. The parechesis expresses the unity of the action, and serves to ‘pile on the agony,’ cp. 12 n., Diph. ii. p. 561 K. (iv. p. 404 M.) γέγηγα καὶ χαίρω τε καὶ πτερύττομαι.

V (ext.) οὕτως παρ’ ᾿Αθηναίοις ἡ παρʼ ἡμῖν N, ἡ νεομηνία, ἐπειδὴ συμβαίνει ἐν αὐταῖς καὶ λήγειν τὴν σελήνην καὶ γεννᾶσθαι" τότε δὲ «τοὺς Α]Ὰ.» μὴ ταχέως ἀποδιδόντας τοὺς τόκους ἢ τὰ δάνεια ἀπήτουν, ὡς ὑπερώρους [-6povs MS., Su.] γενομένους © διὸ λυπεῖσθαί φησιν [R : φασιν Υ] «ὁρῶν R> ἐνισταμένην ἕνην τε καὶ νέαν" πέμπτη (δὲ) αὕτη ἐστιν Ks φθίνοντος [also Su. s.vv. ἕνη τε καὶ νέα].

ἕνη τε καὶ νέα : cp. Plato, Cratyl. 409 B

<n σελήνη φῶς» νέον ἀεὶ ἐπιβάλλει, ἕνον δὲ ὑπάρχει τὸ τοῦ προτέρου μηνός : hence the day before the thin edge of the new moon became actually visible in the evening sky was called ‘old and new’ (ἕνον γὰρ τὸ παλαιόν Su.), when neither the old nor the new moon was visible. Hereby, the approver in Plut. 4Zc. 20 § 5 was convicted of perjury, who stated that he recognized, by the light of the moon, the faces of the Hermocopidae, whose offence was committed ἕνης τε καὶ νέας οὔσης. For ἕνος (=senex) cp. Ach.

n.

For the unpleasant associations of the day cp. Plut. De vit. aér. al. 2 § 2 οὐδʼ ἀναμνήσῃ τῶν καλανδῶν καὶ τῆς νουμηνίας, ἣν ἱερωτάτην ἡμερῶν οὖσαν ἀποφράδα ποιοῦσιν οἱ δανεισταὶ καὶ στύγιον, Hor. S. i. 3. 87; but, at this time, interest fell due on the last day of the month, not on the calends as in Plutarch’s day.

was τις... ois: a construction tabooed in English, except colloquially, but not unexampled in Greek, cp. Aves

## 1136
**πρυτανεῖα** : schol. V (ext.) (ra νῦν καλούμενα παρὰ Ῥωμαίοις σπόρτουλαλ " τὰς γὰρ δεκάτας [-δας R] τοῦ χρέους καταβάλλοντες [ἀπο- R] τοῖς πρυτάνεσιν εἰσῆγον τοὺς χρεώστας [fuller in Su. s.v.]. Both the plaintiff and the defendant deposited sums of money in court (varying according to the amount claimed in the suit) ; these deposits went to the Treasury, but the successful litigant received from his opponent the equivalent of what he had deposited, cp. Vesp. 659 n., Pollux viii. § 38, Isaeus iii. § 47. 3 (with Wyse’s note), [Dem.] xlvii. § 64. It is probable that these fees were peculiar to private actions; on the apparent exception, [Dem.] xliii. § 71, see Wyse 7.6. The confiscated deposits went towards paying the judges, cp. [Xen.] Ath. Pol. 1 § 16. πρυτανεῖα seems to be derived from πρυτάνεις, the pre- Solonian name of the Archons (Busolt, Gr. Gesch. ii.2 p. 157 n. 1, Sandys on [Aristot.] 4th. Pol. 4 § 2).

ἀπολεῖν κἀξολεῖν : a solemn, stereotyped imprecation, cp. Vesp. 1230 n., Dem. xix. 8 172 ἐξώλης ἀπολοίμην kal προώλης.

## 1137
**μέτρια**, δίκαια : a combination which was doubtless familiar in courtharangues, cp. Dem. xxxvii. § 3, Men.

Ἐπιτρέπ. 19 μετρίῳ γε συμπέπλεγμαι ῥήτορι (‘d'une assez jolie force’ Mazon).

Api: cp. Vesp. 795 n. A survival in this phrase (7esp. 186 n.); ἠσί is found in Hermipp. 225 K. (ii. p. 382 M.). The Athenians derived the word from φημί, cp. Kiihner-Blass, Gram. § 289 A. 5.

ἀσπάζομαι, ‘my services to Strepsiades,” ¢ vouchsafe goodmorrow’ (Shak. Cages. 11. i, 313) ‘health, and fair time of day’ (Hen. Vv. ii. 8); a formal address which had now become fashionable, cp.

n., Aves 1377, Plut. 324, 1042.

## 1146
**τουτονί** : schol. V (ext.) θύλακον αὐτῷ ἐπιδίδωσιν ἀλφίτων" kal γὰρ ἄνω [669] εἶπεν ““ διαλῴιτ. κάρδοπον ʼʼ: probably this interpretation is correct, as the humour of the passage requires that the fee should be something characteristically ἄγροικος. In Diog. L. ii § 74 we read that many sent οἶνος and σῖτος to Socrates as ‘remuneration,’ but he sent them back, reserving only a little.

Zielitiski (Glied. p. 45 n. 2) supplies μισθόν, which he believes to have been a talent, this having been the fee paid by Hyperbolus (876) ; but that passage has no bearing on this.

## 1147
**ἐπιθαυμάζειν** : schol. R (not in V) ἀντὶ τοῦ δώροις τιμᾶν (also Su.) ; ‘to gratify’ (Shak. Oth. v. ii. 211) with a ‘remuneration’ (id. LLL. 111. 143 sqq.), ‘condolement’ (Per.11.1. 163). A curious word (possibly a jest κατὰ παρωνυμίαν) which does not recur until Plut. Marc.

## 1510
(=‘toadmire’). The euphemistic sense is readily derived from θαυμάζειν which not only means ‘to wonder at,’ but ‘to show respect to’ (Eur. Med.

8v: referring to the more distant, but more important, antecedent, and not to the “Adikos Λόγος, as Blaydes and Textor (Zur dram. Techn. ii. p. 27 n. 35) think ; see Ran. 576, Biicheler, N. Jahrb, 1xxxiii. p. 675, Dionys. Hal. De comp. pp. 27 sqq. Rhys Roberts.

παρὰ τοῖς ᾿Αττικοῖς" ἄρτι μὲν γὰρ ἀντὶ τοῦ νῦν, ἀρτίως δὲ ἀντὶ τοῦ πρὸ ὀλίγου) [see Su. s.v.]; cp. Vesp. 11 n. and the ancient and modern senses of ‘ presently.’

## 1150

παμβασίλεια : cp. 357 n.

᾿Απαιόλη : schol. R (not in V) ἀποστέρησις ; cp. 729 n. Another instance of Strepsiades’ facility in picking up the scraps from the ‘alms-basket of words,’ ‘the great feast of languages’ of Socrates (Shak. LLL. v. i. 40).

## 1154
Schol. V (int.) ταῦτα [παρὰ τὰ R] ἐκ (τοῦ) Πηλέως Σοφοκλέους [=451 N.2] [Εὐριπίδου, ἐπιφέρει yap ἰὼ πύλαισιν ἢ τίς (ἐνὶ δόμοις R=Eur. fr. 623 Ν.5]. || (μεγάλην, ὑπέροχον, «τὴν ὑπερέχουσαν Su.>. || φησὶ δὲ εἰ οὕτως ὁ παῖς ἐκπεπαίδευται, μέγιστον ὑπὸ χαρᾶς ἐκβοάσομαι [-ἡσομαι MS.] [also Su. s.v. βοάσομαι]. εἴρηται δὲ διαλέκτῳ Δωρίδι). The same words occurred in Phrynichus Com. Satyr: (i. p. 382 K. ; ii. p. 598 M.), cp. Lessing, De Ar. Eur. irrisore, p. 39. For the comic effect of Strepsiades’ lyrical extravagance cp. Plut. 637, 639, Ach. Introd. p. xlviii. (b).

ὑπέρτονον, ‘vent the loud hosanna’; cp. Aesch. Bum. 569 σάλπιγξ. . ὑπέρτονον γήρυμα φαινέτω στρατῷ.

## 1155
**ἰώ**: cp. Ach. 1071 n.

κλάετε: cp. Vesp. 584 τ.

ὀβολοστάται: a term of contempt, like τοκογλύφοι, toculliones (Cic. Ait. ii. 1 § 12), ἡμεροδανεισταί. Though money-lenders, as a class, were not unpopular at Athens (Dem. xxxvii. §§ 53 sqq.), petty usurers, who speculated in distress, were detested (Aristot. Pol. 1. 10=1258 Ὁ 2 εὐλογώτατα μισεῖται ἡ Oβολοστατική) ; so much so, that the fact that a man was a money-lender of this kind was sufficient to prejudice him in the eyes of a jury (Dem. ib. § 52). The name ‘obol-weigher’ means that interest bh was generally calculated monthly by the number of obols paid for every mina; . thus 18 per cent was ἐπʼ ἐννέα ὀβολοῖς (Aeschin. 1, § 107). A petty usurer is called ἀνυπέρβλητος eis πονηρίαν in Antiphan. ii. p. 79 K. (iii. p. 92 M.). ‘When Menippus asks Philonides (Lucian, Menipp. 92) what the Athenians were

engaged in when he died, he is told καινὸν οὐδέν " ἀλλʼ οἷα καὶ πρὸ τοῦ---ἁρπάζουσιν, ἐπιορκοῦσι, τοκογλυφοῦσι, ὀβολοστατοῦσι. For the rates of interest cp. Dict. Ant. i. p. 831 b, E. Caillemer in Daremberg et Saglio, Dict. ii. p. 1216 a.

§ 31, Lysias xii. § 10, Dem. xxiii. § 67, xlvii. § 20, etc. ; see Cuq in Daremberg et Saglio, Dict. ii. p. 752 a.

τἀρχαῖα, ‘capital,’ cp. Plato, Gorg.

## 519
A, Xen. Vect. 3 § 2, Athen. 6126; also τὸ κεφάλαιον.

τόκοι τόκων, ‘use upon use’ (Beaumont and Fletcher, Span. Cur. τ. 1.), a play kal’ ὁμωνυμίαν, as in Thesm. 845 (of the mother of Hyperbolus) ἀξία γοῦν εἶ τόκου, τεκοῦσα τοιοῦτον τόκον. Compound interest (anafocismus) is not often mentioned, but cp. Rhangabé, Ant. Hell. ii. p- 603, E. Caillemer in Daremberg et Saglio, Dict. i. p. 265 b, Biichsenschiitz, Besitz uw. Bewerb, p. 499, CIG. no. 2335.

It was a trait of the μικρολόγος (Theophr. Char. 10) ὑπερημερίαν πρᾶξαι (‘to exercise the right of seizure’) καὶ τόκον τόκου. In itself τόκος was defended by Demosthenes as necessary to enterprise (xxxvi. § 51, lvi. § 1), but it was disapproved of by the philosophers (Plato, Rep. 507 A, 555 E, Legg. 742 c, Aristot. Pol. l.c.), mainly on the grounds crystallized in the mediaeval maxim nummus non parit nummos (Shak. Merch. τ. iii.

ἀμφήκει: as being able to speak equally well on both sides of any

question, his tongue was a two-edged sword (Bacchyl. 10. 87 φάσγανον ἄμφakes) ; hence the reminiscence in Gregor. Nyss. 11. 284 B ἀμφήκεις ἐλέγχους διχόθεν στομώσας.

## 1161
A lyrical senarius, probably from Euripides, who affected resolved feet. In general, choric senarii are strictly constructed, with few resolutions (ZieliYiski, GQlied. p. 293).

πρόβολος : properly ‘a promontory’ or ‘reef’ (Dem. x. § 63, xxv. § 84, Harpocr.), but more commonly in a derived sense, ‘a hunting-spear’ (Herod. vil. 76 Ξε προβόλαιον ib. 148), ‘a champion’ (Ξε πρόβλημα Vesp. 615), cp. Xen. Cyr. v.38 11.

δόμοις : tragic, cp. Ach. 450 n.

βλάβη, “ἃ stone of stumbling’ (like πρόβολος in Dem. Z.c.), ‘an endamagement’ (Shak. John τι. i. 209), cp. Eur. Ion 998 τούτοις τοῖς ἐχθροῖς βλάβος.

Avoavias: schol. V (int.) λύων TAS TOU πατρὸς ἀνίας" ἐκτεταμένως δὲ προενεκτέον [προσ- V] τὴν πρώτην καὶ (τὴν) Ὑ συλλαβήν, a curiously fatuous note ; there is an ‘etymological’ jest (Vesp. 380 n.) here, as the word occurs elsewhere only as a proper name ; but it is tragic in form, and possibly occurred in some tragedy in its etymological sense, ep. παυσανίας (Soph. fr. 801 N.2%) which Su. (not in RV) thinks Aristophanes parodies here.

For λυσ- cp. λυσίκακος (Theogn. 476 B.4), λυσίπονος (Pind. P. iv. 41).

## 1182
**ἡμέραι δύο**: cp. Ach. 159 crit. n. here a part of the phrase εἴπερ. . γε (Ach. 1228 n.). For the combination μή περ cp. μηδέ περ Ach. 222 n., H. Richards, Aristoph. ete. p. 151.

## 1185
**νενόμισται** : cp. 498 n., 1416.

## 1186
**ὀρθῶς**: a word which he has learned in the schools, cp 228 n., 659 n.

νοεῖ : cp. Plut. 55. It is to be noted that arguments ἀπὸ διανοίας τοῦ γράψαντος were permitted in the Athenian courts to an extent unknown in ours. When the language of a law was equivocal, the speaker might argue that the received interpretation was erroneous, or might refer to the motive of the lawgiver, as Demosthenes does xxii. § 30 ἄξιον τοίνυν. . καὶ τὸν θέντα τὸν νόμον ἐξετάσαι Σόλωνα, καὶ θεάσασθαι ὅσην πρόνοιαν ἐποιεῖτο ἐν ἅπασιν οἷς ἐτίθει νόμοις τῆς πολιτείας.

## 1187
Schol, V (ext.) ἀρχή ἐστιν αὕτη

## 1183
**γε**: elliptical (Vesp. 79 n.), not τῆς τοῦ νόμου διηγήσεως. Σόλων δὲ ὁ νομοθέτης, ἀπομιμεῖται (δὲ) τοὺς ῥήτορας τοὺς τὰ ὀνόματα μὲν τῶν νομοθετῶν λέγοντας, μὴ μέντοι [μὴν R] τοὺς νόμους εἰδότας. This scholiast shows a gleam of humour which is rare in their notes.

**γε**, scilicet.

τὴν ἕνην τε καὶ νέαν : by a quibble, Phidippides takes this as if it were τὴν & τε kal τὴν νέαν. For this he had some grammatical justification, as the second article is often omitted in Greek, even with contrasted ideas, e.g. 622 n., Aesch. 4g. 324 καὶ τῶν ἁλόντων kal κρατησάντων δίχα | φθογγὰς ἀκούειν ἐστὶ συμφορᾶς διπλῆς.

Phidippides’ chicanery is an instance of γέλως ἐκ τοῦ ἀδυνάτου (Ach. Introd. Ῥ. 1xv.), and owes whatever plausibility it possesses to a confusion of νέα «σελήνη» with νουμηνία.

ἀπαλλάττοιντο: sc. τοῦ δικάζεσθαι, ‘extricate themselves’ (by means of a compromise) ; cp. Plato, Politic. 304 E, Legg. 915 c, 937 A. In these passages a gen. is supplied (τῶν ἐγκλημάτων, τῆς δίκης), or the sense is helped by διὰ φιλίας ; hence it has been held that there is corruption here, where the sense is not so clear. Legally ἀπαλλάττειν should mean ‘to get rid of a creditor’ (by settling his demands, or by means of a compromise), cp. Isaeus v. § 28, and this is probably the meaning here, with which ἑκόντες is not inconsistent (see Biicheler, N. Jahrb. lxxxiii. p.

## 1195
**ἕωθεν** : viz. the courts were opened very early in the morning (cp. G. F. Unger, ‘Tages Anfang,” Philol. li. p. 33).

ὑπανιῷντο : schol. V (ext.) μετὰ βίας ἀπαιτούμενοι [-otvro R]uacrifowro. © They may wince’ (ὑποκινήσωσι Ran. 644), a curious verb, which is not found elsewhere ; but it is improbable that it is a corruption. Biicheler (XV. Jahrb. Ixxxiii. p- 688. (5)) translates ‘ein bischen chikaniert werden.’

νουμηνίᾳ: the quibble here lies in identifying νέα {σελήνης with νουμηνία. Originally, no doubt, the true new moon was called νουμηνία (Thue. ii. 28 calls it νουμηνία κατὰ σελήνην) ; but as it was observed that the conjunction of the sun and moon rarely occurred at the beginning of the day, the σύνοδος had to be assigned either to the old month or to the new. Thus νουμηνία became the first whole day belonging to the new moon (G. F. Unger in I. Miiller, Hondb. i. p. 563). It is a curious coincidence that, in later days, interest fell due on the νουμηνία, tristes kalendae, cp. 1132 n.

## 1196
Schol. V (ext.) διὰ τί οὐκ ἔμεινε τὸ ἔθος, ἀλλὰ τῇ τριακάδι καταβάλλουσι, δέον τῇ νουμηνίᾳ; Strepsiades does not understand the point, and interprets νουμηνία in its conventional sense, as the first day of the month.

## 1201
Dobree asks why Strepsiades, so dull before, suddenly becomes a Sophist, and speaks of himself as ‘ wise’; he finds the answer to his question in what he calls the ἀσυστασία of the play, which he holds to be due to a want of harmony between the two editions of the Clouds. But the great scholar shows himself an ‘agelast’ here. It is obvious that Strepsiades does not understand a single word of Phidippides’ explanation, which is, indeed, not intended to be an example of clarity of thought; but he is transported by its futile cleverness, although the genuine wisdom of Socrates’ explanations of natural phenomena had left him cold. The satirical meaning underlying the temporary admiration of the quibbling methods of the Sophists should be patent to any student of Aristophanes. Besides, Strepsiades has been to school ; and though expelled as a μισόλογος, he always exhibits considerable cleverness in employing, at unsuitable times, such snippets of Sophistic lore as he had picked up.

**κάθησθε**: an address to the serried ranks of the seated spectators, but also a sly hit at their sluggish intellects, cp. Eg. 396, Ran. 989 sq. In Herod. vii. 140 the priestess at Delphi addresses the Athenian mission

According to Aristippus (Diog. L. ii. § 72) the great advantage of culture was that καὶ εἰ μηδὲν ἄλλο, ἐν γοῦν τῷ θεάτρῳ (οὐδεὶς) καθεδεῖται λίθος ἐπὶ λίθῳ.

## 1222
Some writers (cp. G. Schwandke, De Nub. prior. p. 159) have argued that this scene belonged to the first edition, in which it is supposed that Strepsiades’ education converted him into a perfect Sophist. But such a view shows little appreciation of Aristophanes’ humour. Strepsiades’ assurance here is due, not to his mastery of Sophistic arts, but to his conviction that he has, in the background, in his son a πρόβλημα κακῶν, when he is summoned before the courts to answer for his dishonesty. He consequently feels secure in employing the ‘quips and quillets’ he had picked up in the Pensoir; for subsequent developments, he can fall back upon the assistance of Phidippides. (See Biicheler, N. Jahrb. Ixxxiii. p. 683). There is similar humour in Sganarelle’s ‘orts’ of Deiat lore in Moliére’s Médecin malgreé wi.

**εἰς τὴν ἕνην κτλ**.: cp. 1134 n.

μαρτύρομαι, ‘I protest,” addressed to no one in particular, since, as Isaeus

says (iii. § 1a) τῶν ἀδήλων καὶ ἐξαίφνης γιγνομένων τοὺς προστυχόντας ἕκαστοι μάρτυρας ποιούμεθα ; perhaps, however, he turns the tables on <Pasiasd by addressiug his κλητήρ.

G. Schwandke (De Nub. prior. p. 163) thinks 1228-31 were inserted in the second edition.

## 1233
**ἵνʼ ἄν κτλ**. : schol. V (intramarg. int.) (ἀντὶ τοῦ ὅπου dv σε κελεύσω

ἐγώ). Asv. Leeuwen rightly points out, ἵνα is used in its old sense ‘ where,’ in a legal formula (found in inscriptions only when the verb is omitted, cp. Meisterh. Gram. d. Inschr.! p. 209 § 9). For such survivals cp. Pesp. 186 n.

The place where such oaths were taken was the altar of Zeus, in the marketplace (ὁ λίθος ὁ ἐν τῇ ἀγορᾷ [Aristot.] Ath. Pol. 55 ὃ 5, Dem. liv. § 26, Plut. Sol. 25 § 2, Poll. viii. § 86); but all oaths were not taken here (see Cuq in Daremberg et Saglio, Dict. iii. p. 751 a n. 12), and for this reason Pasias reserves to himself a choice as to where the oath should be taken, as in Lysias xxxii. § 13 περὶ τούτων ἐγὼ ἐθέλω... ὀμόσαι ὅπου ἂν αὐτὸς λέγῃς (Willems, Bull. de 1 Acad. roy. de Belg., 1906, p. 681).

For the custom of swearing while standing on a sacred λίθος cp. Paus. i.

## 38
1,28 §5,iii. 20 § 9, viii. 15 § 2 Hesych. s.v. λιθωμόται, Wilam. Arist. w. Athen, i. p. 46.

τοὺς ποίους θεούς ; cp. Ach. 418 n. The article marks the lively interest which Strepsiades takes in the answer.

## 1234
**τὸν** Ala κτλ. : the custom of swearing by three gods was epic (72. iii. 276 by Zeus, Ge <and rivers), Helios). In Athens it was required by an ordinance of Solon (Poll. viii. § 142), but the deities invoked were altered: Apollo took the place of Helios, and Athena or Demeter of Ge (Wilam. dus Kydath. p. 95). Here Hermes is named, as money is involved (A4ch. 742 n., 779, 816); Posidon, possibly since the loan was employed in the purchase of horses. See further Plato, Legg. 936 E, Dem. xxi. § 198.

νὴ Δία: Strepsiades’ oath ‘by Zeus’ is amusing in this context.

## 1235
**κἂν προσκαταθείην** : schol. V (int.) τουτέστι *‘ καὶ ζημιωθείην [ῆναι R] (ἂν) τρεὶς ὀβολούς, ἵνα ἐπιτρέψῃς μοι ὀμόσαι τοὺς θεούς" οὕτω (γὰρ) καταφρονῶ τοῦ ὅρκου [τοὺς ὅρκους R : τῶν ὅρκων ὅ1.7" θεοὶ γὰρ οὐκ εἰσίν [om. Su.], viz. he offers to deposit, in addition to the legal sacramentum, a whole day’s pay as an Heliast, in order to be allowed to take the oath. Possibly there is a sneer here at the notorious avoidance of oaths by the Pythagoreans, one of whom (according to Iamblichus, Vita Pyth. xxviii. 144) preferred to pay three talents to undergoing the obligation of an oath.

For the form of the expression cp. Plato, Euthyphro 3p, where Socrates says that, so far from charging a fee for his teaching, kal προστιθεὶς ἂν ἡδέως μισθόν, εἴ τίς μου ἐθέλοι ἀκούειν, Theag. 128 A.

## 1236
**ἀναιδείας** : ποῦ impossiby an allusion to the λίθος ᾿Αναιδείας on the Areopagus where oaths were taken (Paus. i. 28 § 5, Zenob. iv. 36).

ἔτι : cp. Pesp. 758 n.

## 1237
The humour of this scene lies in its being an instance of γέλως ἐκ τοῦ δυνάτου kal ἀνακολούθου (Ach. Introd. p- 1xv.), so far as Strepsiades’ replies to Pasias’ just demands are concerned.

Schol. in Su. «ἐπὶ τῶν παραπαιόντων μεταφορικῶς ἀπὸ τῶν κεράμων καὶ τοῦ οἴνου ὅσοις ἅλες ἐμβάλλονται [Kuster : ὅσοι ἅ. β. MS.] ὑπὲρ τοῦ μὴ ἐξίστασθαι, μηδὲ ὀξίζειν εὐχερῶς " ἐπειδὴ τοὺς ὑπὸ μέθης ὥσπερ παραφρονοῦντας ὑποβρέχειν εἰώθαμεν ἐλαίῳ ἁλσὶ μεμιγμένῳ. ὡς οὖν τοῦ Στρεψιάδου μὴ καθεστηκότος ἀλλὰ καὶ παραφρονοῦντος, ὃς ἐπὶ ὡμολογημένοις ἔξαρνός ἐστιν, οὕτω τῇ μεταφορᾷ ἐχρήσατοʼ φησὶν οὖν οὐ τὰ τυχόντα οὗτος (ἂν» ὠφεληθείη διαβρεχθεὶς ἁλσίν». Schol. V (int.) proceeds: ws ἐπὶ κεράμου [R: -ῳ V: om. Su.] ἢ ἀσκοῦ [R: -¢ V] τὸν λόγον ποιεῖται οἵτινες σμηχόμενοι ἁλσὶ βελτίονες γίνονται" ἅμα δὲ ὅτι τοὺς παραφρονοῦντας διαβρέχομεν ἁλσὶ καὶ ἐλαίῳ [perhaps “ὑγθαῦ with clysters’ Ruth.], καὶ ὠφελοῦνται. || (ἄλλως. ὡς παχύδερμον αὐτὸν χλευάζει" τὰ γὰρ παχέα ὑπὸ πιμελῆς τῶν δερμάτων ἁλσὶ μαλαττόμενα [Su. : ματτόμενα MS.] εὐρύτερα γίνονται" ὄναιτο οὖν, φησίν, ἀποκαθαρθεὶς τὴν παχύτητα [also Su.]* ταῦτα δὲ λέγει ἤτοι ὅτι ἀσκὸν ἢ ἀγγεῖον χαλκοῦν βαστάζων.) Few would care to jest if it were their fate to be treated like this. This irrelevancy is as old as Homer (Od. xvii. 225 kal kev ὀρὸν πίνων μεγάλην ἐπιγουνίδα θεῖτο), and is repeated in Plus. 1062.

ἁλσίν κτλ. : the salt was used for tanning, cp. Alex. ii. p. 867 K. (iii. p.

## 471
M.). There may be an insinuation that Pasias was void of ‘the spice and salt that season a man’ (Shak. 77roil. I. ii. 276) if he expected to get back his money. For a similar expression, but a different sense, cp. Ani. 11. Vv. 65 ‘Thou shalt be whipp’d with wire, and ἰδ ἃ in brine, Smarting in lingering pickle.’

διασμηχθείς : σμήχειν in general is Ionic and late, cp. Phryn. p. 253 Lobeck, p. 322 Rutherford, Kiihner- Blass, Gram. ii. p. 538.

οὗτοσί : viz. his stomach, often called ἀσκός, cp. Ach. 1002 n.

**οἴμοι**: an expression of anger, cp. 773, Ach. 590 n.

χοᾶς, ‘’twill hold six pottle-pots’ (cp. Shak. 2 Hen. IV τι. ii. 86). Schol. V (ext.) («ἐπὶ τῶν παραληρούντων, τουτέστιν Su.> ἕξ x. x. ὁ ἐγκέφαλος αὐτοῦ, ἅμα δὲ ὡς ἐπὶ κεράμου ἢ ἀσκοῦ «ἔμεινε Su.) μεταφορᾶς [ἢ ἀγγείου Μ8.1, ὃς ἐὰν σμηχθῇ πλέον χωρεῖ τῶν ἐμπεφρακότων [R: ἐκ- V] ἀποβεβλημένων [R Su. : -βλημένων V] [also Su. s.v. χοᾶ]; cp. Ach. 961 n. As a χοῦς contained 5°75 pints, Pasias’ ἀσκός was of considerable capacity. There is a similar jest in Zhesm. 746

where the ‘affine’ asks I'v. α΄ as to her baby-bottle, πόσʼ ἔτη δὲ γέγονε; τρεῖς χοᾶς ἢ τέτταρας (which is also a jest on Χοᾶς), and in Plaut. Curcul. 110 sqq. PH. sitit haec απ’. PA, quantillum sitit? PH. modica est, capit quadrantal. PA. pol ut praedicas, windemia haec λεία anu non satis est soli.

χωρήσεται : common in the comic frgg. (Mon. 226, iv. p. 346 M., Pherecr. i. p. 187 K.; ii. p. 324 M., Ephip. ii. p. 252 K.; iii. p. 323 M., Dionys. ii

K.; iii. p. 554 M.); also in Plato, Hipp. mai. 288 D τῶν καλῶν χυτρῶν εἰσί τινες δίωτοι τῶν ἕξ χοᾶς χωρουσῶν.

From προίξ ‘a gift, cp. προΐκτης ‘a beggar’ (Hom. Od. xvii. 352), Lobeck ad Phryn. p- 169, Kiihner-Blass, Gram. ii. p. 527.

R] γὰρ τῆς προσηγορίας (ἀκούσας ἘΣ [καὶ] τοῦ Διός, γέλωτα τὸν ἐκείνου τιθέμενος ὅρκον" ταῦτα δέ φησιν, ἐπεὶ δεδίδακται μήτε τὸν Δία μήτε τοὺς ἄλλους ἡγεῖσθαι θεοὺς ἢ τὰς Νεφέλας καὶ τὸν Δῖνον, ὡς ἔφησεν ὁ Σωκράτης.

**ἢ μήν**, ‘1 swear you shall pay me scot and lot for that’ (Shak. 7 Hen. 1V v. iv. 115) ; cp. 865 n., Vesp. 258 n.,

## 1332
Pasias means that, though the vengeance of Heaven is often long delayed (ὀψὲ θεῶν ἀλέουσι μύλοι, ἀλέουσι δὲ λεπτά), he has seen καὶ ἑτέρους ἦσεβηκότας χρόνῳ δεδωκότας δίκην (Lysias

vi. 8 20); but with that he is ποὺ immediately concerned. His business is to recover his money.

## 781
δαιμόνων κτλ. : viz.,, the sons of Carcinus, nicknamed ὁ θαλάττιος ; a parody of such an expression as that in Plato, Euthyd. 291 A ἀλλʼ, ὦ δαιμόνιε Κρίτων, μή τις τῶν κρειττόνων παρὼν αὐτὰ ἐφθέγξατο; (‘some divinity’ and not Euthydemus or Dionysodorus). The allusion here is unknown ; possibly Eg.

## 605
(where Theorus, the jester, of Vesp. 1220 sq., narrates that the Corinthian ‘crab’ complained that he could not escape from the Knights on land or in the depths of the sea), may refer to the incident which is parodied here, viz. a discomfiture in the theatre of Xenocles (nicknamed Datis, Pax 289) who had recently (in 424 B.C., according to v. Leeuwen) produced a tragedy, Licymwius (P. Girard, Educ. athén. p. 285). For the subject of this play cp. Hom. Xl. ii. 661 sq., Pind. 0. 7. 27).

Tlepolemus, the son of Heracles, slew his uncle Licymnius, the brother of Alcmena, and fled to Rhodes, of which he became king, and where he founded the three great cities in the island (Strabo xiv.

## 2
§ 8). Hence he went to Troy, and was slain by Sarpedon (Zi. v. 628 sqq.). According to Fritzsche, δαιμόνων meant, in the original, ‘sons of Posidon.’

. οὗτος λέγει ; ΤΟ. τί δʼ ὅντινʼ εἶπε;

## 1264
Schol. V (int.) ταῦτα Ξενοκλέους ἐστὶν ἐκ τοῦ Λικυμνίου [An- MS.]* λέγεται δὲ ὑπʼ ᾿Αλκμήνης Λικύμνιον [Λη- MS. : -ov Ruth.] τεθνηκέναι [-ὁτος Ruth.] ὑπὸ Τληπολέμου * διὸ καὶ ἐπιφέρει “τί δέ [δαί Ald.] σε T. π. εἴργ. κακόν ;” Ἑϊῤῴφρόνιος παρὰ Ξενοκλεῖ «εἶναί ἘΣ) φησιν [εἶναι R] τὸ χρυσάμπυκες (καὶ Ruth.> παραπεποιῆσθαι τοῦτο" [ἐπεὶ ἐμνήσθη αὐτοῦ ἀπὸ τοῦ πατρός : this is unintelligible]. Homer and Pindar narrate that Licymnius was slain with a stick ; if the scholiast is to be believed, Xenocles described him as owing his death to an accidental fall from Tlepolemus’ car; possibly Tlepolemus was represented as frightening the horses (A. T. Murray, On Parody etc. . 19).

b σκληρέ, ‘discomfortable Fate’ (cp. Shak. Rich. ITI 111. 1i. 36) ; cp. Pax 1250, Eur. Alec. 500, Rhes. 56, Hipp. 871, Andr. 1036.

θραυσάντυγες : in the original χρυσάμπυκες (predicated of the Muses, Pind. P. 8. 89, of Urania, Bacchyl. 5. 13), an unintelligible epithet, but possible in the pseudo-Aeschylean style of Xenocles ;

Schol. V (ext.) διὰ τὸ ἄνω elpyκέναι “ ἀνὴρ κακοδαίμων ” καὶ “ ἰώ μοί μοι" (ἐπήγαγε τὸ ὄντως).

ἐξέπεσον : schol. V (int.) τῶν χρημάτων " ἢν δὲ καὶ οὗτος περὶ ἱππικὴν ἔχων : a jest καθʼ ὁμωνυμίαν, cp. Lysias xxxii.

## 8
10 ἀθλίως ἐκπεπτωκότι {τῶν ὄντων». Amynias was bankrupt (1269 κακῶς πεπραγότι): this was his misfortune which he expresses in the language

T familiar to the race-course, where the overturn of a car was a frequent occurrence (cp. Herwerden, Vind. p. 41).

There is no reason to think that Amynias was really thrown from his car; nor, if he had been, would this have been a reason for demanding back the loan. Strepsiades, however, understands ἐξ. in its literal sense.

V (ext.) πρὸς τὸ ἀπὸ ἵππου ἀπὸ ὄνου εἶπε, καὶ ἅμα πρὸς τὸ ληρεῖς" ἐπὶ γὰρ τῶν μὴ κατʼ οὐδένα λόγον [μηδένα λόγων R] πραττόντων εἰώθασι τὸ ἀπὸ ὄνου λέγειν «κατὰ; τὴν παροιμίαν " τινὲς δὲ ἀπὸ νοῦ λέγουσιν [-ew R]: cp. Pesp. 1370 π., L. Bauck, De prov. etc. p. 23. The quick Greek intellect always saw, in this phrase, a jest kar’ ἐξαλλαγὴν φωνῇ, viz. ἀπὸ νοῦ k. (‘justled from your senses,” Shak. 7p. v. 158); there is a further perversion of the phrase (καθʼ ὁμωνυμίαν) in Eupol. i. p. 357 K. (iL p. 571 M.) ὥσπερ ἀπὸ χοὸς (a jest on ὄνου ‘a bombard,” cp. Vesp. 616 n.)

πεσών (v. Leeuwen). 008] fails to notice that the jest was a ‘Cynical’ one ; cp. Diog. L. vi. § 3 where Diogenes replies to one who wished to know what he needed to be a philosopher “' βιβλιαρίου καινοῦ, καὶ γραφείου καινοῦ, καὶ πινακιδίου καινοῦ" τὸν νοῦν παρεμφαίνων (also ἃ jest of Stilpo, id. ii. § 118); for a similar Cynical’ jest cp. id. vi. 8 52 (Diogenes) ἰδὼν ἱματιοκλέπτην ἐν τῴ βαλανείῳ ἔφη “ὁ ἐπʼ ἀλειμμάτιον ἢ ἐπʼ ἄλλʼ ἱμάτιον.ʼ

**προσκεκλῆσθαι**: the perf implies that the accusation is a fail accompli (cp. Sobol. Synt. p. 106). The use of the present tense, in a prophecy, is similar, cp. Goodwin, M7. §§ 32, 51, Xen. Anab. i. 8 ὃ 12 κἂν τοῦτʼ, ἔφη, νικῶμεν, πάνθʼ ἡμῖν πεποίηται.

## 1
p. 574 K. (ii. p. 1210 M.) ἕτερεγκεφαλᾶν ‘ to suffer from brain-disease,’ Hippocr. dphor. iv. p. 580 L. ἐπὶ πληγῇ ἐς τὴν κεφαλὴν ἔκπληξις ἢ παραφροσύνη κακόν (‘. . are bad symptoms’). There is a curious statement in Athen. 65 F, on the authority of Apollodorus, that the Athenians did not use the word ἐγκ.

ὥσπερ, ‘I think’; cp. Ach. 193 n., Vesp. 395 n., Pax 234, 526, Thesm. 869.

σεσεῖσθαι : a medical term (Ach. 12 n.), especially of the brain, cp. Hippocr. Aphor. ib. p. 594 L. ὁκόσοισιν ἂν ὁ éykéφαλος σεισθῇ ὑπό Twos προφάσιος, ἀνάγκη ἀφώνους γίνεσθαι παραχρῆμα: hence we may translate ‘your pia mater has got a shock’ (cp. Shak. Toil. τι. i. 77).

δοκεῖς : according to v. Leeuwen, redundant after ὥσπερ. But the exagger- “ated expression of uncertainty is intended to raise a laugh, cp. Plato, Phaedr. 260 E ὥσπερ γὰρ ἀκούειν δοκῶ τινῶν προσιόντων καὶ διαμαρτυρομένων λόγων, where Socrates apologizes for his bold personification of ‘arguments,’ . 1277. νὴ τὸν ‘Ἑρμῆν: the God who presided over money-transactions (cp.

## 1279
Schol. V (int.) (ὡς ταῦτα μαθὼν παρὰ Σωκράτους> κατὰ TO σιωπώμενον). This specimen of physical knowledge was not communicated in 368 sqq. ; possibly the scholiast means that he learnt it behind the scenes, during the recitation of the Parabasis (see 627 sqq.). Biicheler (VV. Jahrb. 1xxxiii. p. 671) and Naber (Mnem. xi. p. 319) argue that it may have formed the subject of the problems περὶ τῶν μετεώρων πραγμάτων alluded to in 490, but now omitted ; but it is improbable that that scene was ever longer than it now is (see note ad loc.). The oversight discussed by Biicheler and Naber exists, but it was probably due to the poet, who was not a German metaphysician, and cared little for strict consistency. Joél sees an allusion here to the Cynic practice of applying natural philosophy (as in Plato, Zheaet. 152 πὶ sq.) to the problems of practical life.

**ἀεὶ**. . ἑκάστοτε: cp. 1458.

This reduplication became very common in late Greek, so that in modern Greek a superlative is formed by repeating an adjective (e.g. ὁ μέγας μέγας βασιλεύς, πρωὶ πρωΐ, Jannaris, Hist. Greek Gram. 88 513, 521).

## 1290
According to some writers (e.g. G. Schwandke, De Nub. prior. p. 159), from the first edition, since Strepsiades shows here an ingenuity In argument which is worthy of his son, the finished Sophist. But possibly they have underrated the ability of Strepsiades, who was expelled from Socrates’ school, in reality not because he was an ass, but because the problems submitted to him did not appeal to his practical intelligence.

According to Anaxagoras, the sum of matter cannot increase or diminish (Diels, Vorsokr.? p. 316. 3 sqq.) ; but the problem here is more restricted, cp. Lucr. vi. 607 sqq. For τί δῆτα ; cp. 1105 n.

## 1292
**δίκαιον**: schol. V (ext.) οὐ λυσιτελεῖ τοῖς ἐπὶ γῆς πλείονα εἶναι τὴν θάλατταν. ‘It is not natural that it should exceed its list’ (cp. Shak. Meas. I. i. 6); a curious but not uncommon use which is derived from the epic use of δίκη, as the sum of the conditions (natural or inherited) under which a man lives (cp. Od. xviii. 275, ete.). It is frequently so used in writers on physics, like Hippocr. Περὶ τῶν ἐν κεφ. τρωμ. ἵν. p. 194 L. (in consequence of certain injuries) ὁ θάνατος οὐ γίνεται καὶά γε δίκην, οὐδʼ ἣν γίνηται ‘death is not the natural result’; and in other writers, especially in adverbial expressions, ¢p. 1116 n., Thue. ii. 89 § 3 τὸ δʼ ἐκ τοῦ δικαίου ἡμῖν μᾶλλον viv περι-

ἔσται (as a natural consequence of training), iv. 17 § 5 (H. Weber, Aristoph. Stud. p. 170). Joel thinks the use here may be illustrated by Plut. Sol. 3 § 5 ἐξ ἀνέμων δὲ θάλασσα ταράσσεται" ἢν δέ τις αὐτὴν | μὴ κινῇ, πάντων ἐστὶ δικαιοrary. Hence Thales (Stob. iii. § 79) said: πιστὸν γῆ (as repaying labour, and so teaching τὸ δίκαιον), ἄπιστον θάλασσα, ἄπληστον κέρδος. There may be a parody of the treatment οἵ φύσις as the norm of life, and a sneer at the ethical employment of natural philosophy affected by certain philosophers.

## 1325
**μιαρέ**, ‘stinkard,” scab,’ ‘lousy knave,” ‘ whoreson jackanapes.’

τύπτεις κτλ. : it was a charge against Socrates that τοὺς πατέρας προπηλακίζειν ἐδίδασκε (Xen. Mem. i. 2 8 49), since he taught that, as affection was ultimately based on self-interest, it should bow to it, when there was a conflict between the two, and when a larger interest demanded it. As the passage in Xenophon is held to have a Cynic flavour, Joél argues that Antisthenes is aimed at here, as in the attack on the Cynic view in Plato, Crito, 50 E. He also thinks that Phidippides’ smiling assent to his father’s vituperation is an illustration of the πραότης which Antisthenes recommended in such circumstances, cp. Jr. 109 Mullach παρεκελεύετο τοὺς κακῶς ἀκούοντας καρτερεῖν μᾶλλον ἢ εἰ λίθοις Tis βάλλοιτο: but this may have been ἃ Socratic trait also.

φημί: cp. Ach. 187 n. Notice the calmness of the reply of Phidippides, who, as Mazon well says, has ‘la sérénité d'une bonne conscience logique sur son visage.’

**πατραλοῖα**, ‘ O cankered wretch, kindless, cut-throat dog’ (cp. Shak. Haml. τι. ii. 617). The diphthong is a survival of the old speech in this word and μητραλοίας, while ἀλοᾶν is universal (cp. Vesp. 186 n.).

τοιχωρύχε, ‘brigand,” cutpurse’;

merely abusive, cp. Plu. 909, 1141, Men. Ἐπιτρέπ. 450 ἱερόσυλε γραῦ (like sacrilega in Plautus). For the derivation cp. Vesp. 350 ὀπὴν> διορύξαι, Job xxiv. ‘in the dark they dig through houses which they had marked for themselves in the daytime.” In Ran. 772 sq. the

## 1336
A curious request, since it is obvious that every disputant, even the most unjust, would prefer to support the stronger (or ‘more just’) argument ; the utmost of his profession would be that, if by chance he is forced to support the weaker side, he will convert it into the stronger. In the present case the request is merely a form, and implies that Phidippides is indifferent which side in the dispute his father takes, since, whatever it is, he will prove it to be wrong ; but cp. 115 n.

To be able to sing to one’s own accompaniment was the first condition τοῦ ξυμποτικὸς elvar—at least among oldfashioned people (Vesp. 1220 sqq.).

λύραν : scolia were sung (cp. goat 1356) by a chorus or by individuals, while the lyre was played by the singer, or, more generally, by another guest. κιθαρίζειν : the κιθάρα for a time fell into disuse as compared with the αὐλός (a more passionate instrument), but not for long, as the leaders of fashion, like Alcibiades, soon revolted from it, as being ἀγεννὲς καὶ ἀνελεύθερον, since it spoiled the expression and checked conversation (Freeman, Schools ete. pp. 110 sqq.). Such at least is the tale in Plut. Ale. 2 § 5; but see Aristot. Pol. v. (viii) 6=1341 a 82, P. Girard, Educ. athén. pp. 167 sq.

Such ἐπιμύλιοι δαί, like nurses’ ditties, were often not remarkable for sense, but sometimes they were full of point; see the famous one in PL@. iii. p. 673 B.4 ἄλει, μύλα, ἄλει κτλ., which Thales heard a woman singing over her mill in Lesbos (Plut. Sept. sap. conv. § 14). The names of such songs are collected in Athen. 618 Ὁ ; see Blimner, Technol. i. Ὁ. 32 n. 4. 908] (Der echte Sokr. ete. ii. p. 824) sees a ‘Cynic’ allusion here.

For the sense ‘tetrameter’ cp. 541 n.

ἠρειδόμεσθα, ‘there was old buffeting’ (Shak. iv. 1. iv. 5). ἐρείδειν means ‘to do anything with vigour,” e.g. Eccl. 616 ; of vigorous declamation, Ran. 914; of abuse, 558 n., Eq. 627; of eating, Paz 25. According to schol. Paz l.c., the metaphor is from rowing. Be that as it may, the use is as old as - Homer (11. xxiii. 735 where Achilles said to the wrestlers μηκέτʼ ἐρείδεσθον). The phrase here is imitated by Plut. Qu. conv. τ. ii. 6 § 7 συνερείδοντες ἔπος πρὸς «ἔπος».

ἐπαναπηδᾷ : for the variation of tenses, which is usual in historical narration, cp. Aesch. Pers. 194 sqq., Soph. 4nd.

The alliteration shows the identity of the action, in all its various

phases, cp. 13 n. The Elizabethan synonyms for ‘to beat’ are as varied as the Aristophanic, e.g. ‘to dry-beat’ (Shak. Rom. 111. i. 84), ‘to pun into shivers, as a sailor breaks a biscuit’ (Troil. 11. i. 43), ‘to beat, bob, and thump’ (Rich. III v. iii. 335), ‘to clapper-claw’ ( Wiv. τι. 111. 67), ‘to firk and ferret’ (Hen. V 1v. iv. 29, 33), ‘to scour,” ‘to swaddle’ (Beaum. and Fletch. The Knight ete. 11. iv.), ‘to baste with a hedge-binding ’ (hazel-twig) (ib.), ¢ to vie blow on blow’ (Shrew 11. i. 302), ‘to frush’ (Zrotl. v. vi. 29).

Note that the Anticataceleusmus here is not introduced by ἀλλά (cp. 1351 n.), possibly because the Chorus has no sympathy with the paradoxical argument of Phidippides.

κινητά : Eur. Med. 1317 is curiously similar in expression: τί τούσδε κινεῖς κἀναμοχλεύεις πύλας ; and there may be a reminiscence of that line here, but - scarcely a parody, since κινεῖς is used in its ordinary sense in Euripides, while in Aristophanes κινητής is a comic coinage (Ach. Introd. p. xlix.) for κινητήρ, which is epic and Pindaric; and λογ. poy. is much bolder than ἀναμ. πύλας.

As Christus patiens 121, 437 has kal (or τί) τούσδε κινεῖς κἀναμοχλεύεις λόγους, and Heliod. Aethiop. i. § 8 p. 280 (Didot) τί ταῦτα κινεῖς κἀναμοχλεύεις ; τοῦτο δὴ τῶν τραγῳδιῶν, there is some probability in Porson’s conjecture that the line in Christ. pat. was the original in Eur.

τέθριππον: sc. ἅρμα. “Τὸ keep a fourhorse car’ was a proverbial expression for extravagance, cp. Herod. vi. 125 where Alemeon τεθριπποτροφήσας ʼΟλυμπιάδα ἀναιρέεται, after his accession to wealth ; ib. 35 Miltiades is said to be οἰκίης τεθριπποτρόφου. The possession of horses at all implied wealth in early times, cp. 12 n.

In like manner, Carlyle uses °‘gigmanity’ as an abstract noun connoting, ironically, a certain social standing.

The occurrence of the trimeter in the midst of a series of tetrameters is curious, and has been questioned (see crit. note), but it is justified by the parody (cp. A. T. Murray, Class. Strepsiades has rapidly abandoned the novel vesture of his scepticism for the worn coat of naive Athenian conservatism, against which the Sophistic spirit was a revolt. Possibly (as A. T. Murray, ib. suggests) an echo of Eur.

(Der echte Sokr. ete. ii. p. 824) sees Alc. 683 sq. οὐ γὰρ πατρῷον τόνδʼ ἐδεξάμην νόμον | παίδων προθνήσκειν πατέρας, οὐδʼ Ἑλληνικόν.

Jr. 66 Mullach). There is perhaps a reminiscence of this passage in Plato, Crito, 50 E sqq. Note that Phidippides, by substituting νόμος for νομίζεται, identifies ‘law ’ with ‘custom.’

The instances quoted from Thucydides are irrelevant: i. 90 § 4 (see Classen ad loc.), vi. 15 § 2 (a clear instance of further definition), viii. 64 § 1 (id.), Plato, Rep. 328 Ὁ (id.), 368 B (id.). Here also τὰ βοτά may further define τἄλλα, in the manner of the

journalists, ‘he (Mr. Gladstone) went on to argue.” Be that as it may, τά is defended by the alliteration, which is intended to express a tone of contempt.
